B147|2026-07-29|ALBUM ART COULD ONLY EVER MATCH A PERFECT TAG — the reason B144 and B145 both appeared to do nothing. Two hours after B145 deployed the counters had not moved at all (192 `no_candidates`, 26 `below_confidence`, 0 `hit`), so this was not a short verification window: the pass had drained and matched nothing. Reproducing the EXACT request pharos makes proved the query was fine — `releasegroup:(Always Outnumbered Never Outgu*)` returns `Always Outnumbered Never Outgunned` at MusicBrainz score 100. The failure was in scoring. `pharos_core::match_best` multiplies title similarity by `year_factor`, which returns 0.85 when the year is unknown on EITHER side; this lookup never supplies a query year, so every candidate was multiplied by 0.85 and the effective bar became `title_similarity >= 1.0` — an exact normalised-title match. The 65 hits recorded earlier were exactly the albums whose tags were already perfect. The clipped-tag case B144 and B145 were each written to rescue scores 0.88 and was rejected by a multiplier neither of them touched. SECOND defect, which sent the diagnosis the wrong way for hours: the memo stored `Option<String>`, collapsing every failure into "no candidates", so an album's first track logged the true cause and its other eleven logged one that was never diagnosed — hence 192 `no_candidates` against 26 `below_confidence` when nearly all of them were the latter.|B147/V99. Scoring moves to `best_by_title`, title similarity alone, so `MIN_ALBUM_CONFIDENCE` means what its name says; the floor still rejects a genuinely different album (asserted with the real `Trance Classics` vs `Piano Classics (disc 1)` pair). The memo becomes `Result<String, AlbumArtMiss>` and replays the real reason. Guard test uses the verbatim live MusicBrainz candidates and ALSO asserts `match_best` still rejects them, so the reason for the indirection cannot be quietly lost. `ALBUM_ART_QUERY_VERSION` 4 -> 5.

B148|2026-07-29|AN ALBUM WHOSE ART EXISTS UNDER A SIBLING RELEASE-GROUP STILL RENDERED BLANK. Found in the SAME verification that proved B147 worked (hits 0 -> 103): `The Prodigy - Always Outnumbered Never Outgu` moved from `no_candidates` to `no_cover_art for dd5eafdd`, i.e. the search B144/B145 built finally located it — and then gave up. MusicBrainz routinely holds several release-groups for one album: the live response returned `Always Outnumbered Never Outgunned`, `Always Outnumbered, Never Outgunned` and `Always Outnumbered: Never Outgunned`, ALL at score 100, and the Cover Art Archive has art for only some of them. `title_similarity` normalises punctuation away so the variants TIE — there is no principled winner — yet the code committed to whichever sorted first and reported `no_cover_art` if that one happened to be bare. A coin-flip between a cover and a blank tile, on 31 albums.|B148/V95. `release_groups` returns every candidate clearing the floor, best first, and `album_art` walks them until one actually has a cover (capped at `COVER_ATTEMPTS` = 3, since each is a rate-limited request and past the first few the remaining candidates are ones the floor barely admitted). The memo is written from `album_art` AFTER a cover lands, so a re-run never re-picks a coverless sibling; a transport failure still leaves the memo untouched so an outage is not cached as a verdict. The guard test uses the three real tied candidates and asserts they SCORE IDENTICALLY — the absence of a principled winner is the reason the fallback has to exist, so asserting a winner would have hidden the point (the first version of the test did exactly that and was wrong). `ALBUM_ART_QUERY_VERSION` 5 -> 6.
B149|2026-07-29|ALBUM TILES TOLD EVERY CLIENT TO FETCH A COVER THAT DID NOT EXIST. Seen in the Google TV logcat trace captured for B146: a burst of `coil3.network.HttpException: HTTP 404` per screen of album tiles. `synth_album_dto` stamped `ImageTags.Primary` unconditionally from a hash of the album id, and `synth_primary_tag` (added for the album/artist image work) resolved a representative track without ever checking that track HAS art. `dto::image_tags_for` already states the correct rule for real items and documents exactly this hazard — a video representative can always serve a Primary because a frame is extracted on demand, but an AUDIO track's only Primary source is cached artwork — and the synth path simply never applied it. This is the mirror of B130 (art that could be served but was never advertised) and worse: the client pays for a request per tile and still shows a blank. TWO TESTS WERE ASSERTING THE BUG. `artist_children_are_albums_plus_loose_tracks` demanded a Primary tag from a seed with no artwork at all, and `a_music_album_advertises_the_primary_image_it_can_serve` set `has_primary_art: true` on a `MediaItem` and called `put` — but `has_primary_art` is denormalised and `set_artwork` is its ONLY writer, so `put` silently drops it and that test's track never had art either. Both passed only because the tag was unconditional.|B149/V95. `synth_primary_tag` applies the same audio rule as `image_tags_for`; `synth_album_dto` becomes async and resolves its tag through it instead of hashing an id. Both tests were rewritten to record art via `set_artwork` (the real writer) and now assert BOTH directions — an album with covers advertises, one without does not — stated side by side so neither can be relaxed alone. Two golden files regenerated; the only delta is the removal of the phantom tag.

B150|2026-07-29|OWL CITY'S SINGLE `Fireflies` WAS GIVEN THE ATTIC'S SLEEVE — the wrong-cover failure the confidence floor was supposed to make impossible, reported from production. Cause: the B142 ladder's last rung DROPS the artist (`releasegroup:"Fireflies"` with no artist clause), and B147 made scoring title-only. `releasegroup:"Fireflies"` returns 217 release-groups whose top five are ALL exact-title matches by different acts (Simon Falconer, Still Corners, Katie Garibaldi, Peter and the Wolf, Mike Levy — every one scoring 100), so every candidate scored a perfect 1.0 and the pick was arbitrary. The artist was never verified against anything. Two correct decisions combined into an incorrect one: relaxing the query was right, and scoring on title alone was right, but together they removed every constraint that said WHICH act's album this is.|B150/V100. `Candidate` now carries the `artist-credit` MusicBrainz returns, and `ranked_by_title` rejects any candidate whose credited artist contradicts the file's tag. Permissive where a mismatch is not evidence: an unknown artist on either side cannot contradict, containment accepts `Owl City` against `Owl City feat. Shawn Chrystopher` in both directions, and a compilation placeholder never narrows (MusicBrainz credits the contributors). SECOND HALF, without which the fix could not reach the reported bug: a wrong cover is INVISIBLE to `audio_items_needing_art`, which skips anything with `has_primary_art` — so Fireflies would have kept the wrong sleeve forever no matter how good the matcher got. New `clear_provider_artwork` drops one provider's artwork rows and re-opens those items; `invalidate_stale_album_art` runs it when the stored matcher version differs from `ALBUM_ART_QUERY_VERSION` (6 -> 7), the same wipe-and-regenerate idiom as `TRICKPLAY_GEN_VERSION`. Both directions tested, and the conformance suite covers the new statements against a real postgres.

B151|2026-07-29|THE ALBUM GRID SHOWED `NameGuidPair(name=<artist>, id=<id>)` WHERE THE ALBUM NAME BELONGS. Reported twice before it was placed, because the string looks like a pharos serialisation fault and is not one — no album is named that, no name in the library is blank, and every `Name` pharos emits comes from real tag data (all three checked directly against the production database before touching code). It is jellyfin-androidtv rendering, and the exact line is `BaseItemExtensions.getFullName` at the installed v0.19.9: a `MUSIC_ALBUM` card's title is `artists?.joinToString(", ") ?: albumArtists?.joinToString(", ") ?: albumArtist` followed by the name. `albumArtists` is a `List<NameGuidPair>`, so `joinToString` invokes Kotlin's data-class `toString()` on each element. `SynthItemDto` has no `artists` field AT ALL, so the first branch is null and the second wins.|B151/V98. `SynthItemDto` gains `Artists` as plain strings, set at every site that sets `AlbumArtists` — the two album builders and the `/Items/{id}` synth-album resolution. Guard asserts across `/Albums`, `/Items?IncludeItemTypes=MusicAlbum` and the artist-children rail that any album offering `AlbumArtists` also offers `Artists`, that they are strings, and that both name the same act. Two golden files regenerated; the only delta is the added `Artists` array. METHOD NOTE worth keeping: three rounds were lost guessing from the server side. What placed it was reading the CLIENT's source at the version actually installed — `gh api repos/jellyfin/jellyfin-androidtv/contents/<path>?ref=v0.19.9` — after the user quoted the literal rendered text. A rendered string that looks like a struct dump is a client formatting bug; go to the client's source, not the server's.

B152|2026-07-29|A BLANK ARTIST TAG SHADOWED A GOOD ONE, SWITCHING B150'S ARTIST VERIFICATION OFF FOR THE VERY TRACK THAT MOTIVATED IT. Found by checking the reported item's row after B150 deployed, rather than assuming the fix reached it: Owl City's `Fireflies` carries `album_artist = ''` (present but empty) alongside `artist = 'Owl City'`, and the selection was `album_artist.as_deref().or(artist.as_deref())` — `Option::or` sees `Some("")` and keeps it. Downstream an empty artist reads as "artist unknown", which `artist_is_consistent` deliberately treats as "cannot contradict"; so the track would have gone straight back to matching any of the 217 release-groups called `Fireflies`. The 740-item invalidation had correctly cleared its wrong cover, so it WOULD have been re-matched — wrongly, and looking like B150 had failed.|B152/V100. Tag selection filters blank/whitespace values before the fallback, so a present-but-empty `album_artist` falls through to `artist`. Guard uses a spy resolver to assert the artist the lookup was actually GIVEN, rather than inferring it from a match outcome — the rule under test is tag selection, not matching. `ALBUM_ART_QUERY_VERSION` 7 -> 8 so the affected rows are re-admitted again. NOTE on why the album still resolves: `Fireflies - Single of the Week` vs MusicBrainz's `Fireflies` scores 0.93 via `prefix_containment`, comfortably over the floor, once the query is narrowed to Owl City.

B153|2026-07-29|ASKING FOR A TYPE PHAROS STORES NONE OF RETURNED THE ENTIRE LIBRARY — 51.6 MB of JSON, on every album page open. Reported with the exact request: jellyfin-web's album view asks for the album's music videos, `/Users/{u}/Items?IncludeItemTypes=MusicVideo&Recursive=true&AlbumIds=…`. `MediaKind::from_wire` has no MusicVideo mapping, and every one of the three type-filter sites read "zero recognised kinds" as "no kind filter" (`if !wanted.is_empty() { mq.kinds = wanted }`), so the honest answer — an empty list — became the whole library serialised. "I asked for X" must never widen to "I asked for anything".|B153/V101. `parse_kind_filter` returns a three-way `KindFilter`: `Unset` (no param), `Kinds` (at least one stored kind named), `MatchesNothing` (types named, none of them storable OR synthesised). The nothing case sets the existing empty-but-present `Ids=` shape so the SQL emits `1 = 0`. TWO TRAPS, both caught by tests rather than reasoning: (1) `build_media_query` assigns `mq.filters = f` wholesale further down, so setting the flag at the parse site was silently discarded and the first fix changed nothing — it must be applied AFTER that assignment; (2) a blanket "unknown type means nothing" broke `IncludeItemTypes=Series`, which is answerable by folding Series tiles out of stored episode rows on this very path, so `SYNTHESISED_ITEM_TYPES` exempts the types pharos answers without storing. Guard asserts all three directions: unstored type is empty, synthesised type is still answered, stored type still filters.

B154|2026-07-29|24 ALBUMS HELD A COVER AND STILL RENDERED AS BLANK TILES. `build_synth_image_map` picked an album's (and an artist's) representative track by lowest TITLE, and `synth_primary_tag` — correctly, per B149 — refuses to advertise a Primary for an audio row that has no cached artwork. But the MusicBrainz backfill records the cover against exactly ONE track of the album, and which track that is has nothing to do with title order: alphabetically-first is rarely the matched one. So the tile asked a coverless representative whether it had art, was told no, and emitted no `ImageTags.Primary` while the album's cover sat in the cache. Proved by query against the live DB rather than inferred: 126 albums, 105 with a Primary on some track, only 83 whose lowest-title track held it — a 24-album gap naming `Toxicity (UK CDS2)`, `Hypnotize`, `Morning View`, `Prospekts March (CD EP UK & Europe)` and 20 more. Invisible because both the "no art anywhere" and the "art on another track" cases produce the identical absent-tag wire shape.|B154/V102. The album/artist representative key is now prefixed by whether the track has art (`'0'` with, `'1'` without) before the title — the same `has_primary_art`-first ranking the library tiles in the same function already used — so a group containing any covered track always resolves to a covered one, with title still breaking ties for stability across restarts. Test `album_representative_prefers_the_track_that_has_a_cover` puts the cover on the alphabetically-LAST track and asserts the tag names it; disarmed by dropping the rank prefix, it fails on the absent tag.

B155|2026-07-29|COVERS APPEARED ONLY AFTER A DEPLOY, NEVER WHILE THE SERVER RAN. The synth-image memo (`AppState::synth_image_ids`) caches a synth id → representative item id, INCLUDING negatives, for the whole life of the pod — it exists so a grid of tiles costs one full-library scan instead of one per tile. The metadata backfill, though, keeps downloading album art for hours after boot, and B154 makes the correct representative a function of which tracks have art. So the first render of an album tile froze that album's answer against the artwork that existed at that instant: an album whose cover landed a minute later kept resolving to the coverless track it was warmed with, and one warmed before any of its tracks were even matched kept its negative. The only thing that ever cleared it was a restart, which is why new covers seemed to arrive in a batch on each deploy rather than as the backfill found them.|B155/V103. `AppState` gained an `artwork_epoch` counter, bumped by the backfill whenever a pass enriched anything, and `synth_image_cached` drops the entire memo when the epoch has moved since the map was built — so the next request re-warms against current artwork. Bumping on `enriched > 0` rather than per-artwork-write keeps it to at most one invalidation per pass. Test `artwork_landing_after_the_memo_warmed_re_picks_the_representative` warms the memo on a coverless album, then records a cover and asserts the tag appears; disarmed by removing the `clear()`, it fails.

B156|2026-07-29|OPENING A GENRE ANSWERED 400 `invalid id`. `/Genres` and `/MusicGenres` hand out `genres.wire_id` as the tile id, and every client fetches an item before listing its children — but `fetch_item_dto`'s synth chain resolved library folders, Series/Season, BoxSets, Playlists and MusicAlbum/MusicArtist and then fell through to `parse_item_id`, which rejects a 32-hex id. So the one id shape the genre endpoints themselves emit was the one the detail fetch refused. Found by sweeping three hours of access logs for non-2xx responses rather than from a report: `GET /Users/{u}/Items/689afe218badc80f689afe218badc80f 400`, twice, from Firefox 152 — the id hashes to the genre `Acid Punk`. jellyfin-web does not `.catch` this fetch, so the page simply stopped (the same silent-dead-end shape as B130's missing route).|B156/V104. `synth_genre` resolves a genre wire id against `genres_with_counts()` (no full-library scan on the miss path) and returns the same folder DTO the list builders emit, with `ChildCount` from the row. The `Type` follows MEMBERSHIP rather than the endpoint the client came from — a genre carried only by audio types as `MusicGenre`, anything else as `Genre` — because jellyfin-web picks its detail renderer from `Type` and a music genre rendered as a video Genre lists nothing. Test `opening_a_music_genre_resolves_instead_of_400` takes the id from the `/MusicGenres` listing rather than re-deriving the hash (so a hash change cannot make the test pass while the client breaks); disarmed, it reproduces the live 400 exactly.

B157|2026-07-29|THE ALBUM TILE CARRIED A SLEEVE AND THE PAGE BEHIND IT DID NOT. The grid builder (`synth_album_dto`) resolves `ImageTags.Primary` through `synth_primary_tag`, but the DETAIL fetch (`synth_album_or_artist`, and the artist arm beside it) built its DTO with `image_tags: Some(Default::default())` — an EMPTY map, which serialises as `"ImageTags": {}` and reads to every client as no image at all, exactly the trap `stamp_synth_image_tags` documents for the list path. The detail page has no `stamp_synth_image_tags` call to rescue it. So clicking a covered album tile landed on a coverless header, and the inconsistency between the two surfaces made the art look intermittent rather than absent from one code path.|B157/V102. Both arms now call `synth_primary_tag` — the same lookup the grid uses and the same one that serves the bytes — so the header advertises a Primary exactly when the tile does, and neither advertises one that would 404 (B149). Test `album_and_artist_detail_advertise_the_cover_the_grid_shows` puts the cover on a track and asserts both the album and the artist detail DTO name it; it depends on B154 (without it the representative is the coverless track and there is nothing to advertise from either surface).

B158|2026-07-29|COVER DOWNLOADS TIMED OUT, SO ALBUMS THAT HAVE ART STAYED BLANK. `front_cover_url` asked the Cover Art Archive for `/release-group/{mbid}/front`, which redirects to the archive.org ORIGINAL — a full-resolution sleeve scan, routinely several MB and sometimes tens — and the reqwest client applied a single 20-second WHOLE-request timeout to it. So every cover was pulled at full resolution across a home connection to render a 280px tile, and the slow ones died mid-body. Found by reading the decision log during the B152 re-match rather than from a symptom: `unavailable` was the single largest miss reason (27 consecutive failures on `The Definitive Monkees` alone), and because the reason carried its cause the diagnosis was one line — `provider unavailable: reading cover art body: error decoding response body: request or response body error: operation timed out`. A bare `unavailable` would have sent this back to guessing, which is the whole argument for §"Expose the cause".|B158/V105. The URL now asks for `front-500`, the Cover Art Archive's derived 500px thumbnail — one to two orders of magnitude smaller and still larger than the biggest surface that renders a cover (the detail header). The client's single timeout is also split: `connect_timeout` 10s so a dead host still fails fast, whole-request 45s so a live transfer has room, because one combined budget made an unreachable server and a slow download the same event. No `ALBUM_ART_QUERY_VERSION` bump is needed — `Unavailable` is already excluded from the miss marker (a transport failure is not the item's fault), so the affected rows stayed eligible and retry on the next pass. Test `front_cover_url_asks_for_a_derived_thumbnail_not_the_original` asserts the size suffix explicitly, because dropping it reintroduces the timeouts silently — the bare URL still resolves.

B159|2026-07-29|THE MISS REASON NAMED THE WRONG CAUSE, AND CONTRADICTED ITSELF DOING IT. `ranked_by_title` rejects a release-group on TWO independent grounds — the credited artist is inconsistent, or the title is below `MIN_ALBUM_CONFIDENCE` — but the miss arm reported both as a confidence shortfall, quoting `top_by_title`'s score, which is computed over ALL candidates and ignores the artist filter entirely. So an album rejected because the release-group belongs to a different artist was logged as `best release-group \`Experience\` scored 0.95, below 0.85`: a sentence that refutes itself, since 0.95 is not below 0.85. Found while auditing the 23 albums still blank after B152/B154-B158, where it accounted for 14 of the decision lines. This is a worse failure than a bare reason: a bare `below_confidence` says "I don't know why"; this one confidently sends the reader to lower a threshold that was never consulted, and lowering it would have admitted wrong covers (the exact B150 regression) without fixing a single one of these albums.|B159/V106. The classification moved out of the async fetch into a pure `classify_miss(scored_title, artist, candidates, query)`, which reports `ArtistMismatch { best, want, have }` when the top title CLEARED the floor (so only the artist can have rejected it) and `BelowConfidence` only when the title genuinely fell short. The new variant carries both the wanted and the credited artist, so the next question — is `artist_is_consistent` too strict? — is answerable from the log instead of by experiment. Test `a_wrong_artist_is_reported_as_such_not_as_a_low_score` drives `classify_miss` itself rather than formatting a hand-built variant (the first version of this test asserted only `Display` and passed with the routing disarmed, which is precisely the mistake this bug is about); disarmed, it now reports `below_confidence` and fails.

B160|2026-07-29|EVERY LADDER IMPROVEMENT WIPED THE WHOLE LIBRARY'S COVERS. `invalidate_stale_album_art` keyed its `clear_provider_artwork("musicbrainz")` on `ALBUM_ART_QUERY_VERSION` — the version bumped whenever the SEARCH changes — so a fix that could only ever turn a miss into a hit still deleted every cover already handed out and re-downloaded them at MusicBrainz's one request per second. Measured on the live library this morning: B152 (a blank artist tag no longer shadows a good one) bumped v7→v8 and took coverage from 1243 covered tracks to 727, with roughly an hour of blank tiles before it climbed back past where it started. Nothing about B152 could make an existing match WRONG, so the entire cost was waste — and it was invisible as a bug because the recovery looked like the fix working. Re-admitting misses never needed the clear at all: the miss marker already carries the query version and `audio_items_needing_art` re-admits any row stamped by an older one, touching nothing that has a cover.|B160/V107. Split into two versions with different costs. `ALBUM_ART_QUERY_VERSION` re-admits MISSES and clears nothing (the marker already does the work). The new `ALBUM_MATCH_VERSION` is the one whose change drops cached covers, and is bumped only when a past match may be WRONG — B150's artist check is the example, written because Owl City's `Fireflies` had been given The Attic's sleeve and those covers had to go. An ABSENT record is also no longer treated as a stale one: a fresh install (and the first boot after this split) adopts the version silently rather than reading "never recorded" as "changed" and wiping the library. Tests `a_query_bump_re_admits_misses_without_dropping_covers` and `a_first_boot_adopts_the_match_version_without_clearing`; both were disarmed and confirmed red, the second catching exactly the first-boot wipe this change risks.

B161|2026-07-29|THE BANDWIDTH TEST SENT ZEROS, WHICH WOULD HAVE MADE EVERY CLIENT OVERESTIMATE ITS LINK THE MOMENT ANYTHING GZIPPED. `/Playback/BitrateTest` answered `vec![0u8; n]`. jellyfin-web's automatic quality detection divides the byte count it ASKED for by the wall time to get a bandwidth figure and picks a bitrate from it, so the measurement is only meaningful if that many bytes actually crossed the wire. Zeros compress to essentially nothing: with gzip anywhere on the path, a 3 MB probe goes out as a few KB, the client concludes the link is roughly a thousand times faster than it is, and selects a bitrate the connection cannot sustain — B81's rebuffering reached from the opposite direction. Latent rather than live: nothing on this deployment compressed, which is exactly why it had to be fixed BEFORE B162 turned compression on. Noticed from a user's question about why an album page pulls 1 MB + 3 MB from this endpoint (it is jellyfin-web's normal escalating probe, and not itself a fault).|B161/V108. Two independent guards, so neither one quietly becoming untrue re-opens it: the body is now xorshift64* noise, which no compressor can shrink, and the response carries `Content-Encoding: identity` so actix, angie and any future CDN leave it alone. No new dependency — the generator is eight lines and deterministic. Test `the_bitrate_payload_does_not_compress` asserts the property structurally (distinct byte values and longest run) rather than by invoking a compressor, so it needs no dev-dependency; disarmed back to `vec![0u8; n]` it fails on both counts. `a_short_bitrate_payload_is_still_exact` pins the length for sizes either side of the 8-byte generator stride, because a body off by one byte skews the very figure this endpoint exists to produce.

B162|2026-07-29|NOTHING ON THE HOST WAS COMPRESSED, SO EVERY PAGE LOAD MOVED MEGABYTES IN FULL. The angie pod that fronts jellyfin-web (and reverse-proxies every REST call to pharos) had no `gzip` directive, and pharos has no compression middleware either — so the bundle's 1 MB stylesheet went over the wire as 1 MB, and pharos's list endpoints answered with uncompressed JSON. Reported as "this CSS is 1MB" while asking about an album page. Measured locally against the real bundle and the live server through the same config: the 1 MB stylesheet drops to 349 KB (it embeds base64 assets, so 2.9x is the honest ceiling), and a 500-track `/Items` list drops from 1,059,697 bytes to 83,114 — 12.7x, on exactly the request the library grid makes.|B162/V109. `gzip on` in the shared `mkAngieUi` config, with `gzip_types` as an ALLOW-list of text-shaped types. Deliberately at the proxy rather than as actix `Compress` middleware: angie already fronts every pharos response, whereas an actix compressor would also sit on the video/segment/image paths, where switching a response to chunked transfer breaks the `Content-Length` and `Range` handling those paths depend on — B101 was a size-0 DirectPlay HEAD and B140 a range served whole, both in that machinery. The allow-list also keeps `application/octet-stream` uncompressed, which is what makes the bandwidth probe's measurement survive (B161 hardens the same boundary from the other side). Verified end to end before shipping, not asserted: through the new config the 3 MB probe still reports `Content-Length: 3000000` with no `Content-Encoding`, while the JSON list compresses. NOTE: the deployment pins `pharos-jellyfin-web` to a full commit SHA with `pullPolicy: IfNotPresent`, so this image does NOT roll out until that pin is bumped in the GitOps repo — unlike the server image, which Flux advances automatically from its sortable tag.

B163|2026-07-29|A 213-PIXEL THUMBNAIL REQUEST RETURNED THE FULL 1000x1500 POSTER. `ImageCache::scaled_artwork` skipped scaling for any source under a 256 KiB `SCALE_THRESHOLD_BYTES`, reasoning that a small file already reads fast. But the question a thumbnail request asks is not "is this FILE big?", it is "is this IMAGE bigger than the box it goes in?" — and a 1000x1500 poster compresses to about 253 KiB, slipping under the threshold. `GET /Items/{id}/Images/Primary?fillWidth=213&fillHeight=213` returned 259,639 bytes, byte-identical to the same request with no size parameters at all, and the client decoded a 1.5-megapixel bitmap per tile. Found by a Playwright walk that records every response size across the main views (`payload-audit.spec.ts`), which is also how B153 and B162 would have been caught without anyone noticing a slow page first. On the album grid the caps meant roughly 26 MB where about 2 MB was needed.|B163/V110. The decision now reads the source's INTRINSIC WIDTH from its header — PNG IHDR, WebP VP8/VP8L/VP8X, or the JPEG SOF marker chain — and scales whenever the image is meaningfully wider than the target, whatever it weighs. A 20% band above the target still declines, so a 480-px cap does not re-encode a 500-px sidecar, and scaling UP is never attempted. The parse is a short read of the header, no decode, so asking the correct question per request is cheaper than the byte check it replaces; `SCALE_THRESHOLD_BYTES` survives only as the fallback for a format whose header cannot be read, where guessing wrong risks upscaling. `width_from_header` is split out and tested on byte literals, including the trap that makes a naive scan wrong: DHT (0xC4) sits inside the 0xC0..=0xCF marker range but carries no dimensions, so reading it as a frame header yields a garbage width.

B164|2026-07-29|THE RESUME ROW IGNORED `Limit` AND RETURNED THE WHOLE BACKLOG. `resume_items` read `MediaTypes` off the query string but never `Limit` or `StartIndex`, so it answered with every resumable item however few the client asked for. jellyfin-web's "Continue Watching" home row requests `Limit=12`; on this library it was served 86 items — 401,001 bytes of JSON to render twelve tiles, the single largest API response on the home page. Found by the Playwright payload walk (`payload-audit.spec.ts`) rather than by anyone noticing, because the row LOOKS right: the client simply renders the first twelve and discards the rest, so the only symptom is bytes.|B164/V111. `Limit` and `StartIndex` are honoured, and `TotalRecordCount` remains the UNPAGED total — which is what Jellyfin means by it and what a client would page against; making it the page length would have made the row un-pageable. An absent `Limit` still returns everything, so callers that relied on the old behaviour (the Android TV `/UserItems/Resume` alias sends no limit) are unaffected. Test `resume_row_honours_the_limit_the_client_asked_for` seeds 30 resumable items and asserts both the page size and the untouched total; disarmed, it returns all 30.

B165|2026-07-29|AN HTTPS-ONLY DEPLOYMENT ADVERTISED ITSELF OVER PLAIN HTTP. `/System/Info/Public` and `/System/Info` returned `LocalAddress: http://prototype.aliflix.alison-jenkins.com` — the right host, the wrong scheme — and `derive_local_address` is the URL clients are told to use when publishing a reachable address to peers (casting, SyncPlay, native clients). Anything that followed it dropped to plaintext, or was blocked outright as mixed content on an HTTPS page. pharos was NOT at fault: it reads the scheme through actix's `ConnectionInfo`, which honours `X-Forwarded-Proto`. The angie config in front set `proxy_set_header X-Forwarded-Proto $scheme`, and since TLS terminates at the cluster gateway and angie's own listener is plaintext, `$scheme` is `http` — so the proxy overwrote the gateway's truthful `https` with its own. Isolated by sending the header to each hop in turn: straight to pharos with `X-Forwarded-Proto: https` returned `https://`, the same request through angie returned `http://`. The same clobber fed every other `connection_info().scheme()` caller — `dlna::base_url_from_request` and the remote-client check's sibling helpers.|B165/V112. angie now honours an inbound `X-Forwarded-Proto` via a `map` and falls back to `$scheme` only when the header is absent, so a deployment with nothing in front still describes itself correctly. Fixed in the shared `mkAngieUi`, so the pharos-ui pod gets it too. Verified against the live server through both configs before shipping: new config + `X-Forwarded-Proto: https` gives `https://`, header absent gives `http://`, old config with the header gives `http://`. Test `the_advertised_address_follows_the_clients_scheme` pins the SERVER half of the contract in-tree, because the proxy half lives in `flake.nix` and has no test harness — the half that can regress silently is the half a repo test can hold.

B166|2026-07-29|EVERY IMAGE HEAD ADVERTISED `Content-Length: 0`. `deliver_image` ended its HEAD path with `.finish()`, an empty body. actix's h1 encoder derives a HEAD response's `Content-Length` from the body's declared `BodySize` and DROPS any manually-inserted header, so `BodySize::Sized(0)` put a zero on the wire for every poster, thumb and backdrop. Spotted while inspecting an unrelated image request: `content-length: 0` sat next to `etag: "266a6-18c5d36c7e4e9ef1"`, whose own prefix is 157,350 in hex — the response was declaring nothing while its ETag described a 154 KiB file. This is B101 exactly, on the image path rather than the video one; there a zero-length HEAD collapsed Firefox's `<video>` seekability probe to `buffered`, so the class already has a demonstrated cost for any client that sizes a fetch from HEAD.|B166/V113. The HEAD path now returns a `SizedStream` declaring the file's real length. Its stream is never polled for a HEAD, so no bytes are read off disk — the response stays as cheap as `.finish()` was while telling the truth. The test asserts the DECLARED `BodySize` rather than the header, because the header is stamped by the h1 encoder at wire time and the in-process test harness never materialises it — asserting the header would have produced a test that passes on a bug (it reads `None` either way). Disarmed to `.finish()` it reports `Sized(0)`.

B167|2026-07-29|A DEPLOY EVICTED THE TWO PEOPLE WHO WERE QUIETLY WATCHING AND SPARED THE ONE PRESSING BUTTONS. A rolling update at 19:21:43 drained the pod, which closed all three `/socket`s in a live watch party ("draining — closing /socket so the client reconnects to the new pod"). The new pod hydrated the group at 19:22:13 with every member `connected=false`, `last_seen` stamped now. No client came back quickly: there was not one `/socket` request in the log — not even a failed one — until 19:33:48, and the other two returned at 19:43:34 and 19:43:46. Reconnects took 12, 22 and 22 MINUTES against a `DISCONNECTED_TTL_MS` of 600 s, so at 19:32:13 — exactly 600 s after hydration, to the second — the ghost prune removed alison and lace mid-film. The third member survived only because that person kept pressing Ready, and every attributed command refreshes `last_seen`: liveness was inferred from ACTIVITY, and watching quietly produces none. The eviction was also silent (B168), so the two clients kept playing against a group that had moved on, which is what the room experienced as "some of us got out of sync". The grace had already failed this way once — T83 chose 150 s, a member was evicted 12 s before its client reconnected, and the answer was to raise it to 600 s.|B167/V114. The verdict moved off the member and onto the GROUP. A disconnected member is no longer pruned individually at any age: it holds no readiness gate (`follower_ids` filters on `connected`), so retaining it costs a roster line while evicting it costs someone their evening — and no duration can separate "closed the tab" from "reconnecting", because the only evidence either produces is silence. What is still reaped is a group where NOBODY holds a socket and nothing has been heard from anyone for `GROUP_ABANDONED_MS`, which dissolves WHOLE; B29's guarantee that orphaned snapshots do not haunt the join picker is preserved through it. The dead-socket prune stays, narrowed to its actual justification — a member we still believe holds a socket but which has gone silent does block every gate to the anti-wedge timeout. Instrumentation shipped first and separately: `pharos_syncplay_socket_gone_seconds{cause}` measures the reconnect gap that both graces were guessed at without ever recording, `pharos_syncplay_members_awaiting_reconnect` makes retention a number rather than an absence of log lines, and the prune log now carries `silent_ms` and the `ttl_ms` it was judged against. The old test `a_disconnected_member_that_never_returns_is_still_pruned` asserted the behaviour that broke the party and is replaced by its inverse; disarmed to the old rule it fails with `left: ["stayed"]` — the button-presser surviving alone, which is the incident.

B168|2026-07-29|THE EVICTION WAS NEVER MENTIONED TO THE EVICTED. After B167's prune removed two members, their clients reconnected at 19:43:34 and 19:43:46 and the server logged "syncplay: /socket connected + registered in hub" and said nothing else. jellyfin-web keeps its own `groupInfo` and stays in SyncPlay mode until told otherwise, so both players carried on free-running against a group that had moved on. The first either user learned of it was eleven minutes after the eviction, when one pressed pause and the command path answered `NotInGroup` (B24, doing its job — but only for someone who happens to press something). Rich-on-success, silent-on-failure: the reconnect path that FINDS a membership sends a full `Joined` + catch-up, and the one that does not send nothing at all.|B168/V115. `ResyncMember` for a member the roster no longer holds now sends `ServerMsg::GroupLeft`, which stock jellyfin-web routes to `disableSyncPlay` — the client exits SyncPlay cleanly and can re-join, instead of pretending. Deliberately narrow: the obvious version, answering EVERY group-less socket connect with `NotInGroup`, is wrong, because `Manager.processGroupUpdate` maps both `NotInGroup` and `GroupLeft` to `disableSyncPlay(true)` and that toasts "SyncPlay disabled" — an unconditional send would fire on every page load for every user. Verified by reading the deployed bundle rather than assumed. This fires only where the server holds the contradiction: an attachment to a group that does not list the member. Counted by `pharos_syncplay_stale_membership_healed_total` so a recurrence is a rate, not a report from the sofa.

B169|2026-07-29|EVERY FILM WAS DATED BY WHEN IT WAS COPIED, NOT WHEN IT CAME OUT. "Lara Croft - Tomb Raider - The Cradle of Life" (2003) displayed as 2026, `PremiereDate` 2026-07-29 — the day it was looked at. 139 films in the live library carry a 2025 or 2026 year, among them "2001: A Space Odyssey" as 2025-07-07 and "Apocalypse Now" as 2025-05-06. The signature that settles it: "300" and "300 - Rise of an Empire" are both dated 2026-07-19, "Avatar" 2026-07-20, "47 Ronin" 2026-07-21 — consecutive days, which is an acquisition timeline rendered as release dates. Both probe backends read the container's `creation_time` tag as a release date; it is the time the FILE was muxed. The libav backend (the deployed default) aliased it into `date`, so one tag poisoned `production_year` and `premiere_date` together, and since the `embedded` provider outranks `filename` (30 vs 10) it also beat the `(2003)` sitting in the filename. The online match was never at fault — the item carries the right TMDB id, overview and rating (5.762, exactly what the UI showed) — but `apply_enrichment` fills only empty fields on the "local always wins" rule, so a mux date already in the row locked the real date out permanently. Reported as "films keep getting the wrong year", with a screenshot showing 2026 beside a correct 5.8 rating: the pairing is the diagnosis, since a wrong match would have got both wrong.|B169/V116. `creation_time` is removed from the date sources in BOTH backends, reversing the fallback B90 added; a genuine `DATE`/`originaldate` tag is still read. `PROBE_SCHEMA_VERSION` 5→6 so the incremental scan re-probes and the affected rows drop the mux date, after which the metadata backfill fills the real year from the provider it had already matched correctly (admission is TTL-based, so no manual re-admit). The libav tests use REAL ffmpeg-generated fixtures rather than a hand-built tag map, because the defect was in WHICH container keys get consulted — precisely what a mocked map assumes instead of testing; ffmpeg stamps `creation_time` into an MP4 unprompted, so the trap is present without asking for it. Disarmed, both backends report `Some("2026-07-29T11:04:12.000000Z")` where `None` is required. Instrumentation shipped first and separately: `pharos_metadata_field_source_total{field,provider}` records which of the four providers actually supplied each dated field — the merge is first-Some-wins and nothing recorded the winner, which is why a wrong year and a right one looked identical.

B170|2026-07-30|EVERY BOOK WAS UNOPENABLE, AND CLICKING PLAY DID NOTHING WHATSOEVER. 004-books emitted `Path` only when the client's `Fields` asked for it, matching how the field behaves for every other kind. jellyfin-web ships three book readers — `bookPlayer` (epub.js), `comicsPlayer` (libarchive.js), `pdfPlayer` (pdf.js) — and each one's `canPlayItem` is a test on `item.Path`'s extension. The item they test is the one the DETAILS page fetched, and that request sends `/Users/{uid}/Items/{id}` with **no `Fields` at all**; the `Fields=CanDownload,Path` seen in the bundle belongs to the episode download button, which is what the original research read it as. So `Path` was absent exactly where it decides, `canPlayItem` returned false for all three readers, and `playbackManager` declined: no iframe, no `/Items/{id}/Download` request, no console error, nothing in a server log. The failure had no observable symptom of any kind — which is the class the whole feature was written to defend against, built into the defence. Found only because the acceptance test was automated in real Chromium instead of being called a manual step; before/after on the same fixture: iframes 0→1, reader containers 0→3, chapter-one marker false→true, `/Download` never requested→requested.|B170/V117. `Path` is emitted unconditionally for `MediaKind::Book` and stays `Fields`-gated for every other kind. Also fixed alongside: `BaseItemDto` had no `CanDownload` field at all despite the readers asking for it by name. Tests split accordingly — `a_book_always_carries_its_path` asserts `Path` on BOTH an unfielded list and `/Items/{id}` (the exact route that was broken), while `path_is_absent_for_a_non_book_unless_the_client_asks_for_it` keeps the gate honest for a movie; the V69 case-spelling test moved onto the movie, because run against a book it would pass no matter what `fields_requests` did. Disarm-verified (`false &&` on the book clause → 2 red). Golden diff inspected before regeneration: exactly 368 `"CanDownload": true` additions and no `Path` leak into any non-book payload.

B171|2026-07-30|THE BOOK DOWNLOAD ROUTE WOULD HAVE ADVERTISED A ZERO-LENGTH HEAD. `/Items/{id}/Download` is how all three readers fetch a book's bytes, and a HEAD that answers `Content-Length: 0` tells a client there is nothing to fetch. This is B166/B101 on a third route: actix's h1 encoder derives the header from the body's `BodySize` and discards a hand-set one, so any `.finish()`-shaped HEAD path reproduces it. Caught in review rather than in production because V113 already named the class — the value of writing the invariant down.|B171/V113. The route is built on `NamedFile`, whose `SizedStream` makes the declared length structural rather than something a later edit can drop. Asserted at the `BodySize` level, not the header, for the reason V113 records: the in-process test harness never materialises the header, so a header assertion reads `None` either way and passes on the bug.

B172|2026-07-30|EVERY PART-WATCHED FILM REPORTED 0% PROGRESS ON THE ROUTE THE GRID RENDERS FROM. `BaseItemDto::from_domain_with_user_data` built its `UserData` through `UserItemDataDto::from_domain`, whose runtime argument is a hardcoded zero, while holding an item whose `run_time_ticks` had been computed two lines above. B36 had fixed exactly this for the session and user-data routes and left the ITEM routes — `/Items` and `/Users/{uid}/Items/{id}` — untouched, so a card's resume bar drew empty for a film watched half through. The value was always right there; it simply was not passed. Found by a 004-books test asserting that a BOOK reports 0% honestly (no runtime, so no percentage): the counterweight film assertion, present so an "always 0" implementation could not pass, went red instead.|B172. `from_domain_with_user_data` now passes the item's own `run_time_ticks`. A book's runtime is 0, which yields 0.0 rather than a NaN — serde writes NaN as `null` and a client draws a null percentage as a full or broken bar, which reads as corrupted state rather than as an absent duration. Goldens moved by exactly one field: 58 `PlayedPercentage` values from `0.0` to a real non-zero, with no other field disturbed.

B173|2026-07-30|ONE CORRUPT PDF WOULD HAVE TAKEN THE SERVER DOWN. `lopdf` 0.34 overflows the stack on deeply nested PDF objects (RUSTSEC-2026-0187, CVSS 7.5), and 004-books put it on the scan path — which parses whatever a user drops in a media folder, so the input is untrusted by construction. A stack overflow ABORTS the process: it is not a `Result` any caller can handle, so V6 ("a bad file is logged and skipped; the scan continues") cannot hold against it, and on the deployment the scanner runs in-process with the server. Never reached production — CI's cargo-audit failed the PR, which is the entire reason that job exists.|B173/V6. Upgraded to lopdf 0.44 — not the 0.42 the advisory asks for, because 0.42 and 0.43 depend on `ttf-parser` unconditionally and that crate is itself flagged unmaintained (RUSTSEC-2026-0192), which `cargo deny check` treats as an error where `cargo audit` only warns. 0.44 makes it optional, so with default features off it leaves the graph entirely rather than being tolerated by an ignore entry — pharos reads the page tree, the info dictionary and image XObjects, and never asks lopdf to touch a font. 0.42+ also drops the parser feature flags 0.34 forced. `a_deeply_nested_pdf_does_not_take_the_process_down` builds a PDF holding 50k nested arrays and requires the reader to return at all; nextest's process-per-test means a regression surfaces as a CRASHED test rather than a failed assertion, which is the only honest signal for a class that by definition cannot be caught. Disarm-verified by pinning back to 0.34: SIGABRT, "has overflowed its stack". `embed_image` stays off and the `image` crate is confirmed absent from the lock, so R11's no-raster-decoder premise is unchanged.

B174|2026-07-30|EVERY TYPED LIBRARY WAS EMPTY AND ALL 14,229 ITEMS SAT IN ONE MIXED PILE. `backfill_library_ids` stamped `library_id` by iterating `libraries()` and letting each root overwrite the last, on a stated assumption: "Longest-root-wins is not needed (roots don't nest in practice); a later root simply overwrites if it does." They do nest — a whole-share `mixed` root beside typed libraries for its subfolders is the obvious way to configure this, and it is exactly what the deployment does. `libraries()` orders by NAME, `media` sorts after `Books`/`Movies`/`Music`/`TV Shows`, so the containing root was applied LAST and swallowed everything inside it. Live: `media|mixed|14229` against `Movies|0`, `Music|0`, `TV Shows|0`. Nothing errored; the library simply rendered as one undifferentiated grid, which looks like a configuration choice rather than a defect. Found while adding a `books` library for 004-books — the config change would have silently done nothing, which is how the underlying bug surfaced at all.|B174/V120. Roots are now applied SHORTEST-FIRST in both backends, so the most specific library has the last word; ordering by name was never meaningful for assignment, only for display, and `libraries()` keeps its name order for that. Test `a_nested_library_beats_the_root_that_contains_it` reproduces the exact ordering trap — the containing root is deliberately NAMED so it sorts last, because a test whose names happened to sort the other way would pass against the bug — and asserts the third case too: an item directly under the mixed root and in no typed library must still be claimed, or the fix trades one silent gap for another. Disarmed, the Books library comes back `[]`, which is the production symptom exactly.

B175|2026-07-30|OPENING A BOOK TOOK BETWEEN 1.3 AND 35 SECONDS, AND NONE OF IT WAS THE SERVER. Reported as "the performance of opening the book is pretty awful". pharos was exonerated first, by measurement: a 24 MB Kindle book converted COLD and delivered in 160 ms TTFB / 384 ms total in-cluster, 223 ms through the public ingress, 8 ms warm; the converted EPUBs are structurally sane (26 chapter parts, compressed, no giant blob). The cost is entirely in the reader jellyfin-web ships. `bookPlayer` renders the book, hides it behind `opacity: 0`, and keeps the spinner up until `book.locations.generate(1024)` resolves; epub.js 0.3.93 — the version jellyfin-web 10.11.8 pins, and still the newest release — ends `Locations.process` with `this.processingTimeout = setTimeout(() => completed.resolve(…), this.pause)` and defaults `this.pause = pause || 100`, which jellyfin-web never overrides. So every book pays a flat 100 ms of IDLE WAIT per linear spine section before anything becomes visible. Measured against six real library books at **101.1 ms per section**, invariant to file size (a 26-section 24 MB book opened faster than a 30-section 159 KB one) and unchanged by `--disable-background-timer-throttling` — a sleep, not work. Deployed: The Docker Book 1.3 s (12 sections), The Prince 3.1 s (30), The Way of the Web Tester 12.7 s (121), Learn D3.js 35.1 s (342). Worst affected are NATIVE `.epub`s, not the converted Kindle ones. No server-side signal could ever have shown this: the request the reader makes completes in milliseconds and the wait begins after it.|B175/V124. `jellyfinWebBundle` in `flake.nix` rewrites the constant to 0 in the epub.js chunk before the bundle enters the image, keeping the `setTimeout` so each section still yields to the event loop and the tab stays responsive. Measured 4.6-5.6x faster to open across the same six books: 1.3 s→0.29 s, 3.1 s→0.59 s, 12.7 s→2.5 s, 35.1 s→6.2 s. The rewrite asserts exactly one matching file and exactly one matching site, then asserts BOTH post-conditions (old constant gone, zero pause present) and fails the build otherwise — the identifier is minifier-chosen, so a nixpkgs bump can rename it, and a patch that silently matched nothing would restore the full slowness with every test still green. `the_served_epub_js_has_no_per_chapter_sleep` covers the other half, that the bundle actually served is the patched one, which a `bundle = pkgs.jellyfin-web` regression would break without troubling the derivation; pointed at stock upstream it fails with `"100"`. The devShell's `JELLYFIN_WEB_DIR` was moved onto the same derivation so the compat suite exercises what production serves.

B176|2026-07-30|MARKING SOMETHING UNWATCHED LEFT IT RESUMING FORTY MINUTES IN. `op_unmark_played` set `played = false` and left `last_played_position_ticks` untouched, so an item could report `Played=false` beside a live resume point. jellyfin-web draws the resume bar and the Continue Watching row off the POSITION, not the flag, so a card marked unwatched still showed a progress bar, still sat in Continue Watching, and still started part-way in when played. Jellyfin's own `MarkUnplayed` clears both. Surfaced sideways: the 004-books `/Download` spec began timing out after the epub.js patch (B175) sped the reader up, because the page-turn spec before it left a read position on the same seeded book and `bookPlayer` resumed to chapter two — the flag and the position are one answer to "have I seen this", and a mutation that moves one and not the other leaves the pair contradicting itself.|B176. `op_unmark_played` clears the position too. The test drives the position through `/Sessions/Playing/Progress` — the route a client actually uses, so the value survives the same path it does in production — and asserts on both the item's `UserData` and on Continue Watching, the surface the stale position was visible on. Disarmed, it fails with `left: Number(50000000)`. The books spec now clears the position itself rather than inheriting whatever the previous spec left, so its precondition is stated instead of assumed.

B177|2026-07-31|EVERY COLD SEGMENT WAS 3.4x SLOWER THAN IT NEEDED TO BE, BECAUSE PHAROS COMPETED WITH ITSELF. Found by profiling live playback rather than from a report. One client segment request launches itself plus six speculative prefetches, and all seven were admitted to the single GPU at once; the client's own segment then got a seventh of the encoder. Measured on the deployment, same file, same rung, idle GPU: with the prefetch window already warm one job runs alone and the segment costs **1 860 ms**; with the window cold, seven jobs are admitted and the same segment costs **6 358 ms** (mean encode across the seven: 6 229 ms). `queue_wait_ms` and `lock_wait_ms` were 0 in every observed case and `transcode_ms ≈ encode_ms`, so none of it was queueing — the cost was inside ffmpeg, competing with work nobody was waiting for. B108's `background_headroom` was aimed at exactly this and cannot reach it: it reserves a PERMIT, which is what a client's segment needs to start, and says nothing about the encoder throughput it needs to finish. The same profiling showed cost is decode/IO-bound, not encode-bound — 360p costs the same as 1080p for a 16x smaller output — so the crowding is not recovered by serving a cheaper rung.|B177/V125. A speculative job now skips a device already carrying a client's segment plus `background_alongside_client` (default 1) of its own kind, and tries the next device or is shed. Deliberately not zero: prefetch is shed rather than queued, so what is refused is never retried, and starving it entirely would make every segment a cold miss — the opposite bug, which `speculative_work_may_fill_an_idle_device` pins. Instrumentation shipped first and separately: `JobDone.peer_jobs` and `pharos_transcode_peer_jobs{class,device}` record what each job encoded BESIDE, because `encode_seconds` rising cannot distinguish a heavier source from a crowded device and the two want opposite fixes. The fix test asserts what joined the CLIENT'S device rather than what was admitted at all — a prefetch on an otherwise idle second device costs the client nothing — and disarmed reports 3 joining instead of 1.

B178|2026-07-31|A JOB DISPATCHED OFF THE QUEUE REPORTED ZERO PEERS, HOWEVER CROWDED THE DEVICE WAS. `place()` stamped `ctx.peer_jobs` at dispatch (B177); `try_place_no_queue()` — the path a queued job takes when a permit frees, called from `drain_pending` — never did, so a job that had to wait reported `peer_jobs = 0` and recorded no `peer_jobs` span field regardless of what it landed beside. Jobs only queue when every permit is busy, so the drain path IS the crowded path — the instrumentation was blind in exactly the case it exists for. It also left a hole for a later admission controller: a drained interactive job would feed it `background_peers = 0`, silencing its back-off guard exactly when the device is busiest. The task's own proposed test (one GPU permit, a second job queued behind it) turned out not to prove any of this: `DeviceSlot::new` clamps ANY probed capacity to at least 1 ("a device with a probed cap of 0 would be useless"), so the ever-present CPU permit let the second job dispatch there immediately instead of queuing at all (confirmed empirically — it landed on `DeviceId::Cpu` with `queue_wait_ms == 0`). And even corrected to force a real queue, "the drained job ran alone" makes the expected `peer_jobs`/`background_peers` both 0 — exactly what `JobCtx` already carries as its zero-initialised default before any dispatch path touches it, so a `try_place_no_queue` that stamps nothing would have passed that version too.|B178. Both `place()` and `try_place_no_queue()` now stamp `ctx.peer_jobs` (via `peers_on`) and the new `ctx.background_peers` (via `background_peers_on`, the `JobClass::Background` subset) immediately before the job joins `state.inflight` — so a job running alone still reports 0, but a crowded one reports true company regardless of which path dispatched it. Both go on the `transcode_job` span (declared `tracing::field::Empty` beside `peer_jobs`) and on `JobDone`. Test `a_job_dispatched_off_the_queue_reports_its_peers` keeps two jobs alive on a 2-permit GPU (one Background) plus a filler consuming the always-present CPU permit, so the queued job (B) is forced to actually wait and then drains onto a device that still has one live (speculative) peer — `peer_jobs == 1`, `background_peers == 1`, neither reachable by an unstamped default. Disarm-verified: deleting the two stamping lines from `try_place_no_queue` fails the test with `left: 0, right: 1`; restored, it passes. Guarded by V125 — the allowance it feeds is only as good as the peer count it is computed from.

B179|2026-08-01|PHAROS ASKED A GPU TO DECODE A CODEC IT HAS NO DECODER FOR, AND HAD WRITTEN DOWN THAT DOING SO WAS SAFE. A 10-bit AV1 episode (`Supergirl - S01E06`, `codec_name=av1 profile=Main pix_fmt=yuv420p10le`) wedged on every attempt: three jobs dispatched to `Nvenc:0` within the same second, `device_in_use{Nvenc:0}=3`, `segment_produced_total` absent, no cooldown, no progress over 45s. `DecodeAccel::of` emitted `-hwaccel cuda` for ANY job placed on an NVENC device — the source codec was not an input to the decision and `TranscodeOptions` did not even carry it — on the strength of a doc comment stating, as a measurement, that with the device present but the codec unsupported by its decoder ffmpeg "falls back to software and exits 0, which is why an odd codec on a real GPU is safe". The deployment's GTX 1070 is Pascal: H.264/HEVC/VP9 decode blocks, no AV1 one. ffmpeg 8.1 did not fall back — `hwaccel initialisation returned error`, `Decode error rate 1 exceeds maximum 0.666667`, exit nonzero, 100% of frames lost. Software decode of the same file benchmarks at 13.1x realtime on 4 threads, so nothing about the source was slow or broken; only the decoder it was handed was wrong. The claim was probably measured on VAAPI, where the software fallback does happen, and never re-tested on NVDEC. Unreachable before spec 007: shared-init fMP4 never went to hardware, so browser playback decoded AV1 in software and played — 007 made hardware reachable and the false comment became load-bearing the same day.|B179/V137. The verdict comes from evidence instead of the assertion: `probe_decodable_codecs` runs a real trial decode per `SourceCodec` per device at boot (software-encode a five-frame 128x128 sample, then decode it with the device's own `-hwaccel` flags, `-hwaccel_device` included) and the answer lands on `DeviceSlot::decodable`; `decode_offload_for` asks it once at DISPATCH, because the answer is a fact about the (source codec, device) PAIR and the device is not known until placement; the result rides `JobSpec::decode_offload` because the argv is built in a separate PROCESS with no capability table, which is the gap the old code fell through. An unnamed source codec denies the offload — guessing yes wrongly kills a rendition, guessing no wrongly costs a software decode. `source_video_codec` is `#[serde(skip)]` so it reaches neither the wire nor `RenditionKey`; merely clearing it in `RenditionKey::new` was NOT enough, serde still emitted the key and the pin still moved, which would have re-placed two thirds of the live fMP4 renditions on deploy (caught by two placement tests, not by design). The false comment is corrected in place rather than deleted: left standing it justifies reintroducing this. `pharos_transcode_decode_offload_total{device,verdict,source_codec}`.

B180|2026-08-01|ONE UNDECODABLE FILE TOOK THE WHOLE GPU OUT OF SERVICE AND KILLED 453 CLIENT SEGMENTS OF UNRELATED TITLES. The same incident as B179, but this is what turned a broken rendition into an outage. `classify_failure` decided between error classes on ONE input — whether the job ran on a hardware device — so B179's decode failure became `DeviceBusy`, the only transient class and the only one that sets a cooldown. Under 007's shared-init pin a cooled device does not slow a rendition down, it FAILS it: `candidates_for` records `Invalidated` rather than spilling to a second encoder (V130's accepted cost), so for the cooldown window every rendition banded to that card failed. Measured over one process: `pharos_transcode_device_cooldown_total{device="Nvenc:0",reason="device_unusable"} 4`, 74 interactive and 453 coalesced_failed segments. The card was healthy throughout and encoding other titles fine either side of each window. The cause was invisible twice over — `DeviceBusy` was a unit variant that discarded the ffmpeg tail (fixed the same week), and even after it carried one, nothing counted a failure that did NOT cool a device, so the cooldown counter was the entire signal for a failed job.|B180/V138. `WorkerError::DecodeFailed` splits the two verdicts about the same hardware and is deliberately NOT transient — the offload decision is made per (source, device) before dispatch, so an identical retry elsewhere fails identically. Classified on two strings that name the decode side explicitly, checked BEFORE the hardware arm: ffmpeg's `Decode error rate N exceeds maximum M` and NVDEC/VAAPI's `hwaccel initialisation returned error`. `pharos_transcode_job_failed_total{device,reason}` closes the second blind spot the fix would otherwise have opened: with only the cooldown counter, a rendition could now fail every segment forever without moving a single series.

B181|2026-08-01|THE MPEGTS RUNG PUBLISHED IMMUTABLE SEGMENT URLS WITH NO SOURCE GENERATION. Found by 008's end-to-end test rather than by reading the code — the first thing that test caught. `rendition_qs` appends `s=<srcgen>` so a re-resolved URL-backed source cannot keep serving the previous resolution's bytes out of a browser cache held `immutable` for a year (V132), but `render_variant_playlist` reached for `playback_qs` instead and emitted neither `g=` nor `s=`. The reasoning that led there is sound and incomplete: the mpegts rung repeats its parameter sets per segment, so it is genuinely exempt from the `#114` shared-init hazard `g=` exists for, and the exemption was read as covering the whole query string. It does not — `serve_segment` hands `.ts` files back `public, max-age=31536000, immutable` exactly like every other segment, so V132 applies to that rung whether or not `g=` does. Harmless for a local item, whose bytes cannot move; for a URL-backed one it means a year of a superseded resolution in the browser with no way to reach it.|B181/V132. `with_source_gen` holds the append rule in ONE place, used by both `rendition_qs` and a new `source_qs`, because the rule has a trap on each side: omitting `s=` outlives the bytes, applying it unconditionally changes every LOCAL segment URL and busts a warm cache plus every held init for an item that cannot re-resolve. The test pins both directions, including that a local mpegts URL does not gain `g=` either.

B182|2026-08-01|EVERY OTHER SEEK FROZE THE ROOM FOR EXACTLY THIRTY SECONDS. Every other gate type resolved in 2-4s, so the seek gate alone was the fault. A seek gate inherited `READY_TIMEOUT_MS`, which is sized for a client LOADING a new item — measured real acks reach 18.1s while it fetches an init segment and fills a buffer. A seek loads nothing: the item is already open, and when the target sits inside the buffered range the player has nothing to transition THROUGH, so jellyfin-web emits no `Ready` and the gate is unsatisfiable from the instant it opens. The mechanism was not new — B137 established it for the unpause gate and bounded that one to 5s — it was simply never applied to its neighbour. Both members sat flagged buffering and neither ever acked, exactly the case B137 named as "waiting 30s on a member that has no way to answer is not patience, it is a hang".|B182/V139. `SEEK_GATE_TIMEOUT_MS`. The dividing line is NOT who opened the gate but whether a member must LOAD AN ITEM to satisfy it: `QueueChange`/`NextItem`/`PlaylistItem` genuinely do and keep the long bound; `Seek` does not. `Restored` keeps it too, because a gate rebuilt from a snapshot cannot know which it was.

B183|2026-08-01|A GROUP CREATED AROUND AN EPISODE ALREADY PLAYING COULD NEVER START A JOINER. Reported as "sync play doesn't seem to be aware of existing video playback" — the observation was right and the mechanism was the opposite: pharos IS aware and seeds the queue from the creator's live session every time (`seeding new group from creator's now-playing item`, item and position both correct). The seed is what is insufficient. It deliberately sends NOBODY a `PlayQueue`, so that a creator mid-episode is not reloaded, and its own log records the cost as `creator_bound=false`. The consequence for the JOINER was never worked through: no client holds the server-minted `playlist_item_id`, so the joiner is handed a queue nobody else can follow. Live evidence, three joins in 90 seconds into a seed-only group with not one `/syncplay/ready` from the joiner, against the same member joining a broadcast-backed group and readying in 5 seconds. The operator's workaround was to destroy the group and restart playback inside a fresh one — which is a real `SetNewQueue` broadcast, i.e. the thing the seed declines to do.|B183/V139. A second member arriving promotes a seed-only queue to a real broadcast, which is the moment the seed's rationale ("do not disturb the creator") stops being free. The creator's player reloads once, at the instant it invited somebody, landing at the position it was already playing. Deliberately NO readiness gate on the promotion: a gate there would wait on members with nothing to transition through, which is B182.

B184|2026-08-01|TWO SECONDS OF VIDEO ON REPEAT FOR A MINUTE. A healthy freeze the same session read `held_ms=530 outcome=recovered`. The unpauses came from the buffering member itself, and jellyfin-web auto-fires `Unpause` about a second after a queue change, so no human need press anything. B137 introduced the supersede for good reason — a frozen member's player has no transition left to make, so it can never post the Ready/BufferingEnd that clears its own flag, and the V19 guarantee was to be "preserved by re-report". Re-report does work; when the member has not recovered it works immediately, which is a loop rather than a recovery.|B184/V139. A freeze that BEGAN within `REFREEZE_LOOP_WINDOW_MS` of the last supersede is proof the previous supersede was premature, and that repeat is declined. Not a blanket refusal: the FIRST supersede is B137's escape hatch and is untouched, since a member holding a stale flag has no other way to say so. Not a hang either: the freeze still ends on genuine recovery and is anti-wedge bounded. `pharos_syncplay_unpause_declined_total{reason}`.


B185|2026-08-02|A CLIENT CRASH WAS THE FIRST DIAGNOSTIC ANYONE HAD OF A ROTTEN LIBRARY. The Google TV app died 2.8 s into `Neon Genesis Evangelion - S01E25`. The real defect is that this was unknowable in advance: nothing in pharos ever read a media file past its header, so a file with an intact header and a hole in its middle probed perfectly, entered the library looking healthy, and surfaced as a mid-playback failure with no server-side signal to point at. The scan's own memo (V134) records files that fail to PROBE; there was no equivalent for files that probe and then break. Related but distinct from the 134 dead files already memoised — those fail at the header and are caught; these pass it.|B185/V140. A whole-file demux-only integrity sweep on the libav worker pool, paced behind the `bg_io` gate and memoised per `(mtime, size)` so a library is read once, not once per pass. `pharos_media_integrity_total{verdict}` (clean/damaged/short/unreadable/error), `pharos_media_damaged_files`, `pharos_media_integrity_seconds`. The damaged-file log line carries the path, the position of the first fault and the demuxer's own words for it. The three corrupt Evangelion episodes need re-acquiring; the sweep is what makes the rest of the library answerable without waiting for the next crash.

B186|2026-08-02|A SOLO CREATOR'S PLAY BUTTON DID NOTHING, AND THE SERVER LOOKED HEALTHY DOING IT. Reported live: "my sync play group watching iron man was paused, now I cannot unpause it". The cause is one line 2 minutes earlier: `seeded group queue ... creator_bound=false`. The group was created while Iron Man was already playing, so `SeedQueue` filled server state and deliberately broadcast nothing (not reloading a creator mid-film is the whole point of the seed). No client therefore holds the server-minted `playlist_item_id`, so a group command has no queue entry on the client to apply against. B183 had already fixed this shape — by promoting the seed to a real broadcast when a SECOND member arrives — but a creator watching ALONE never gets one, so the promotion never fires and the group stays unbound indefinitely. The fix was scoped to the reported symptom (a joiner who could not start) and the solo case was left behind it.|B186/V139. `controls_existing_queue` — Unpause/PauseShared/SeekTo/LeaderPlay/LeaderPause/LeaderSeek promote a seed-only queue BEFORE they run, via the same `promote_seed_only_queue` the join path now shares. Queue-REPLACING commands are excluded: they broadcast a queue themselves, so promoting first would only send it twice. Issuing a group playback command is an unambiguous request for group behaviour, so it earns the same one-off reload the join path already accepts. `pharos_syncplay_queue_promoted_total{trigger}` (join|command) — a promotion is now a countable event rather than an inference from an absence.

B187|2026-08-03|A RESUME PLAYED THE FIRST TIME AND NEVER AGAIN, AND EVERY SERVER SIGNAL SAID IT WAS FINE. Reported as "Lace could not watch Supergirl". The same title, the same user, the same browser: at 15:15 it played for 48 minutes; from 17:59 on, eight attempts started nothing. Every server-side signal was green — `pharos_segment_produced_total{outcome="ok"}` only, no `pharos_source_unreadable_total`, no WARN on the playback path, PlaybackInfo responses identical in shape, the pod 42 hours old across both. The only visible difference was in the CLIENT's behaviour: the working session fetched 10 segments a minute (1x, real playback) and posted `/Sessions/Playing` plus 755 progress reports; the failing ones fetched ~60 a minute, drained 45 minutes of video in seven, and posted no playback start at all. The cause was 12 bytes. The demuxed audio rendition serves one `init.mp4` resolved by `resolve_audio_file`, which takes it from the DEEPEST session that has one, documented as safe because "the init is codec configuration and is identical across sessions". It is not: ffmpeg writes any session built with `-output_ts_offset` — every SEEK session — an EMPTY EDIT in its edit list equal to that offset (`elst` gains a second entry: `media_time = -1`, `segment_duration` = the offset; reproduced exactly, 730 vs 742 bytes locally, 766 vs 778 in production). Every fragment is ALSO re-anchored onto the absolute timeline by B121's `shift_tfdt`, so a player honouring the edit applied the offset twice: audio at 7524 s in a 6484 s film, never overlapping the video's buffered range, so hls.js kept loading and the element never reached a playable state. The 15:15 session worked because it was the day's first — no seek session had written an init yet, so the neutral from-0 one answered. It left an `s338` session behind, and every attempt after it drew that init instead. Position was not the variable: the 18:12 attempt resumed at segment 335, beside the 338 the working session played from, and still failed.|B187/V141. `fmp4::drop_empty_edits` strips every empty edit from the audio rendition's init and fixes the enclosing box sizes; `vp9_audio_file` runs it on every init served. Dropping the edit rather than the `tfdt` shift is deliberate — the shift is what B121 proved works across players, and edit-list handling is the part of MSE clients disagree about. The result is the byte-identical init the whole-file session would have written, which is the property the serve path needs and not merely "no empty edit": the URL is served `immutable, max-age=1y` and names nothing about which session produced the bytes, so two sessions differing by one byte leave one of them in a browser for a year describing fragments it does not match. Tested against real ffmpeg output at both layers (helper converges byte-for-byte on the from-0 init; the ROUTE serves a neutral init after a deep seek), each verified red when disarmed. `pharos_audio_init_total{shape}` (neutral|deferred) and a WARN carrying the offending deferral and the session that produced it are the signal the failure had none of — the whole diagnosis was reconstructed from a client's request RATE, because nothing said which session's init a client had been given.

B188|2026-08-05|THE FIX FOR B187 PUT THE AUDIO AHEAD OF THE PICTURE. B187's `drop_empty_edits` removed EVERY empty edit from the audio rendition's shared init, on the reasoning that the only thing writing one was a seek session's `-output_ts_offset`. Two things write one. A WHOLE-FILE session pads the front of a late-starting audio track on purpose (`-af adelay`, B120) so its fragments keep the track's true start, and ffmpeg records that pad as an empty edit as well — so the fix silently undid B120, the exact defect of audio running ahead of picture for an entire title. Caught by the instrumentation shipped alongside it rather than by a user: `pharos_audio_init_total` fired 95 times in 20 minutes carrying `session_start_seg=0, empty_edit_secs=0.011`, and a from-0 session with a deferral of its own is a contradiction under B187's model. Reproduced at 1.7 s against a deliberately late audio track. The tests did not catch it because their fixture's audio starts at 0, so its whole-file session had no pad to protect — the suite could not distinguish "removed the session's offset" from "removed everything".|B188/V141/V142. `shrink_empty_edits(init, session_offset_secs)` subtracts a caller-supplied amount instead of zeroing, and `vp9_audio_file` passes the PRODUCING session's own `session_start_seg * AUDIO_SEGMENT_SECONDS` — 0 for a whole-file session, so its init is untouched by construction rather than by a guard. A seek session keeps the residue left after the subtraction: `-ss` lands on a packet boundary rather than exactly on the 6 s grid, and that is a real measurement of where the audio begins relative to it. Since the two bodies are now legitimately different, `resolve_audio_file` takes the init from the SHALLOWEST session rather than the deepest, so which one a client gets is deterministic (the URL is `immutable, max-age=1y`); a seek session still answers when no whole-file session exists, because a mid-file track switch spawns none (B106). Both new tests drive a genuinely late audio track and were verified red against the unconditional strip while the seek-side tests stayed green.
B189|2026-08-15|PLAYING A FILM TO ITS END RETURNED 500. The last segment of a title whose AUDIO outruns its VIDEO could never be produced. `SegmentGrid` — and so `SegmentWindow`'s tail clamp — is built from the CONTAINER duration, while the encoder stops at the end of the VIDEO stream; where those differ the final window asks for video the source does not hold. `short_of_frames` applied its `out_time < want * 0.9` rule to that window like any other, called the result incomplete, retried with a 45 s decode preroll that cannot conjure video that does not exist, and failed identically. Live: Ant-Man (media 479628986076528820, container 7026.771 s), `/videos/…/h264cmaf/1171.m4s` → 500 on all 14 attempts across 7 distinct play sessions, `reached only 0.500s of the requested 0.773s`. Deterministic, permanent, and not load-related — the retry was pure cost. Found because a SyncPlay joiner's player landed on that segment and so hit it on the FIRST request of every session (T114) rather than at the end of a film, where nobody had reported it. The same class was fixed once before by clamping the tail window to the media duration; that clamp is against the container, which is precisely the number that is too large here.|B189/V144. `short_of_frames` takes `is_final_segment` and, when set, skips the DURATION rule and nothing else — the zero-frame rule still runs, because the source running out explains a short segment and never an empty one. The flag comes from `SegmentWindow::is_final`, set by `SegmentGrid` when the index is the last of the grid and by nothing else: the window's fields are private precisely so a delivery path cannot state a position of its own, and a path that could declare its own segment "final" could switch the completeness check off for any segment it liked. `false` when the duration is unknown (no grid, so no last index) and for an over-index (outside the grid, not the tail of it); `#[serde(default)]` for the worker IPC boundary, where absence reads as "not the tail" and keeps the strict rule. Written test-first: `the_final_segment_is_accepted_when_the_video_track_ended_early` fails against the old rule with the exact production string. The narrowness is guarded by its neighbours — `a_short_segment_that_is_not_the_tail_is_still_rejected` and `a_final_segment_with_no_video_frames_is_still_rejected` — and `only_the_last_window_of_the_grid_is_final` pins which windows carry the flag. NOT fixed by adding a video-track duration to `MediaProbe`: that is the quantity the check really wants, but it costs a `PROBE_SCHEMA_VERSION` bump and a full re-probe of a 13k-item NFS library, and would leave the 500 live until that finished.
B190|2026-08-16|AN UNMAINTAINED ARCHIVE PARSER WAS READING UNTRUSTED FILES. `sevenz-rust` 0.6.1 — the `.cb7` comic reader's decoder — had its repository DELETED (RUSTSEC-2026-0246) and carries an unfixed relative/absolute path traversal in `decompress_impl` (RUSTSEC-2026-0245, CVSS 8.3) that lets a crafted archive write outside the extraction root. Never exploitable in pharos: the reader uses `Archive::read` + `SevenZReader::from_archive` + `for_each_entries` and pulls each entry into memory, so no entry name is ever joined to an output path. Both advisories were therefore first held on reachability, with `a_cb7_is_never_extracted_to_disk` guarding the claim against a future call. Surfaced by CI failing a branch that had nothing to do with archives — cargo-audit on -0245, then cargo-deny on -0246, which it escalates from warning to error (the same asymmetry as B173).|B190/V145. Migrated to `sevenz-rust2` 0.21.4, the successor named by the advisory. `ArchiveReader::new` replaces the `Archive::read` + `from_archive` pair and parses the header itself; `archive()` returns it by reference, preserving `read_7z`'s promise that a page count costs no decompression and the file is opened once. Default features OFF: the successor defaults to `bzip2`, a C library, and the same objection keeps `.cbr` permanently cover-less (R7/R11) — only `compress` + `util`, dev-dependency only, for the fixture builder. Both ignores and the guard test are deleted rather than kept: a guard whose only purpose is to stop a suppression going stale outlives its reason once nothing is suppressed. `cargo audit` and `cargo deny check` both exit 0 with the entries removed, which is the difference between absent and silenced.
B191|2026-08-16|EVERY TITLE'S LAST SEGMENT REPORTED FRAME LOSS THAT HAD NOT HAPPENED. The B189 fix made the tail SERVE; this is the same wrong premise one layer up.
