Frame accounting compares `produced_frames` against `SegmentWindow::expected_frames`, and that expectation is derived from the container-clamped window while the encoder stops at the end of the video stream — so Ant-Man's segment 1171 logged `expected_frames 19, produced_frames 15, deficit 4` and incremented `pharos_segment_frames_total{result="short"}` on every cold encode, measured on the live deployment immediately after B189 shipped. Not an outage; two corrupted signals. The WARN promised "a hitch at this boundary" for a boundary with nothing after it to hitch into, once per title-end forever — and a warning that is always wrong is one the reader learns to skip, taking the true ones with it. The counter was worse: `short` is the rate you query when hunting the silent frame loss of B-series/V107, and it had a floor of one per title-end.|B191/V145. New `result="tail_deficit"` label, and the WARN suppressed for that case alone. Deliberately NOT folded into either existing value: `short` is false because the frames never existed, and `complete` is the worse lie, because a tail that lost frames for a real reason would become indistinguishable from a healthy one — so the exemption covers the WARN only and the count stays queryable under its own name. The decision moved out of a long async fn into `FrameResult::{classify,warrants_warning}` so the rule is testable at all; `is_final` still comes from `SegmentGrid` (B189), so no caller can claim the exemption for an arbitrary index. Disarm-verified by forcing `is_final` false.
B192|2026-08-16|A RECONNECTING MEMBER WAS EVICTED BY A DEAD SOCKET'S TEARDOWN, REPEATEDLY, ALL EVENING. Spider-Man: Homecoming group watch: alison's seeks and pauses did nothing and the group had to be recreated three times (14:43:13, 14:43:52, 14:50:19). `RECONNECT_GRACE` schedules a teardown 20 s after a socket disconnect, fenced on the device's connection generation so a reconnect within the grace no-ops it. The generation lived on the hub's `SessionEntry`, and `remove_if_current_gen` REMOVES that entry — so the counter reset, and every registration after an eviction started at 1: precisely the value some earlier connection's pending teardown still held. Measured: disconnect→removal was 20.002 s on all four occurrences, and the device ids were byte-identical across every reconnect (so NOT the varying-deviceId trap of B82/T114). Self-perpetuating — each eviction guaranteed the next registration started at 1 again — which is why creating a new group never helped: the eviction keys on the device, not the group. The damage outlived the eviction, because removing the hub entry also makes a LIVE socket unresolvable: `hub.resolve` returned None while the client's socket was open, so 19 later commands hit the no-socket drop path (B193).|B192/V146. Generations now come from a hub-wide `AtomicU64` taken before the entry is touched, so a value is never reused whether or not the entry survived a teardown. Shared across devices deliberately — the number is only ever compared with itself, so a global counter costs nothing and makes reuse unrepresentable rather than merely unlikely. `a_generation_is_never_reused_after_the_entry_is_removed` replays the live sequence (remove at gen N, re-register, stale teardown at gen N) and fails against the old per-entry counter at the `second.gen != first.gen` assertion.
B193|2026-08-16|A SYNCPLAY COMMAND THAT REACHED NOBODY WAS ANSWERED "204 NO CONTENT". 204 is success, and jellyfin-web fires a SyncPlay command and moves on, so a dropped Seek is indistinguishable from an applied one — the user scrubs, nothing moves, and no client-visible surface says why. Exposed by B192: once a member was evicted from the hub, 19 commands (13 unpause, 7 seek, 4 pause) took the `no /socket registered and no persisted group` path, every one answered 204. The only symptom available to the people watching was a scrub bar that did not move. B24 had already established the rule for the ADJACENT path — a live socket with no group is told `NotInGroup` over that socket — but the branch with no socket at all kept falling through to the shared `no_content()` at the end of `dispatch`.|B193/V146. Both genuinely-dropped paths now report: no deviceId is 400 (can never be routed; retrying cannot help), no live socket AND no persisted membership is 503 (caller did nothing wrong, condition is transient — the socket may reconnect and re-attach). The `NotInGroup` path deliberately KEEPS its 204 and its existing assertion, because there the client is told over its own socket; the distinction is whether any surface other than the status line remains. `a_command_that_reaches_nobody_is_not_reported_as_success` pins it.
B194|2026-08-16|A GROUP'S POSITION GREW WITHOUT BOUND, AIMING JOINERS PAST THE END OF THE FILM. The group derives position as `anchor + wall-clock elapsed` and re-anchors only on an explicit Play/Unpause/Seek, which is correct while playback advances at 1x and false the moment it does not — it counts through the film ending, every client closing its tab, or a local pause whose command never reached the server (B192/B193 were doing exactly that). Nothing bounded it because `QueueEntry` held only ids: the actor could not know how long the item was, so it could not know it had run past the end. Measured 2026-08-15T21:06Z by the V143 hydrate signal, on its first recurrence after being added for exactly this: `anchor_age_ms=13349164` (3 h 42 m) aiming a joiner at `position_ms=20337295` (5 h 39 m). That joiner's player clamped to the final segment and 500'd there on every retry — the original T114 report, whose fatality B189 had already removed.|B194/V147. `QueueEntry` carries `runtime_ms`, resolved server-side by mapping over the same `item_ids` vec handed to the group (alignment by construction; a short vec degrades to unknown rather than misassigning). Every read of the position clamps to it — including `send_playback_state`, which computes its own value and is the path that actually places a joiner's playhead: the clamp went into `current_position_ms` first and the joiner test still failed until this second path was fixed, which is why the test drives the hydration path rather than the accessor. Past the end by more than a 10 s grace, the group settles Paused at the end rather than counting forever; the grace exists because a probed duration is not exact and settling on contact would pause a group early on a rounding difference. `None` runtime (unresolvable item, or a snapshot predating the field) leaves the position unclamped exactly as before. Counted as `pharos_syncplay_settled_past_end_total`.
B195|2026-08-16|A BURNING RENDITION LANDED ON THE CPU WHILE THE GPU SAT IDLE. Black Panther (media 4956781705437454830), two viewers, subtitles switched on mid-playback. Turning subtitles on mints a new burn rendition (`burn=true, burn_idx=0`), and that rendition drew the CPU band of the weighted placement spread: every burn segment ran on `cpu` (`transcode dispatch candidates: [Cpu] excluded: []`) while `pharos_transcode_device_in_use{Nvenc:0}` stayed 0 of 8 capacity for the whole session. Burn re-encodes every frame through a filter graph and the CPU's 4 permits are also where all audio, copies and every speculative prefetch land, so the margin was thin: burn encodes measured 3.2-4.7 s per 6 s segment, and the client was taking coalesced hits at 1.6-3.2 s, i.e. arriving at each segment at about the moment it finished. CORRECTED SCOPE: this entry first claimed the resulting 23.7 s stall as its own, reading `seg 87 queue_wait_ms 23711` as scheduler starvation. It was not. In the same instant a `hit_path=fast` CACHE READ of an already-present file recorded `read_ms 23712`, and `tokio::fs::read` of a local-path PVC cannot take 23.7 s because a transcode queue is busy — the whole process froze, and every in-flight measurement recorded the same freeze. That freeze is T117, and it is what stopped the picture; this entry is the thin margin that left nothing to absorb it.|B195/V148. `DeviceTable::rendition_device` now prefers hardware for a rendition whose `burn_subtitle_stream_index` is set, when any hardware device encodes the codec. NOT a load-aware pin — that would re-place a rendition mid-playback and reintroduce #114, which the function's purity exists to prevent. The bias reads only `opts`, fixed for the rendition's life, so the same rendition still resolves to the same device on a fresh table and under cooldown (both asserted). A preference and never an eligibility gate: with no hardware for the codec the full set stands, or subtitles would be unplayable on a CPU-only box. The weights themselves were not wrong — capacity 8 against 4 already gave NVENC two thirds of the bands — the rendition simply drew the other third, and burn is where that draw hurts. `HLS_GEN_VERSION` 16 -> 17, mandatory: `SegmentIdentity` carries no device, so burn segments cached under 16 would otherwise be served beneath an NVENC-produced init. This widens the margin; it does not fix T117.
B196|2026-08-16|A ROLLING DEPLOY SPLIT ONE RENDITION ACROSS TWO ENCODERS AND A VIEWER'S PLAYER FROZE ON TWO FRAMES. Deploying B195's burn-placement change (`HLS_GEN_VERSION` 16 -> 17) during a live watch: the new pod booted 19:42:20, found the marker stale, wiped the shared cache tree and claimed generation 17; the OLD pod was still alive on the generation-16 rule (burn -> CPU) and wrote segments 712-716 back into the paths just cleared, while the new pod encoded 717+ on NVENC. Confirmed from the logs — one rendition `(burn=true, codec=26, burn_idx=0)` with `devices used: {cpu, Nvenc:0}` — and the viewer stuck at 1h11m36s, exactly segment 716, flickering between two frames because libx264 and NVENC emit incompatible SPS (#114) and the init she held could not decode the other encoder's bytes. The generation guard was the right idea in the wrong shape: a wipe-at-boot marker protects a RESTART, where there is one writer, and a RollingUpdate guarantees two. Deleting the five poisoned segments server-side did NOT recover her, because segments are served `Cache-Control: immutable, max-age=1y` (V132) and her browser kept serving itself the cached copies; only a hard reload cleared it. That is the second half of the lesson — a generation bump cannot recover an in-flight client, because the corruption happened AFTER the bump, inside the same generation, under URLs that stayed valid.|B196/V149. The cache root is now the generation's own subtree (`{base}/{generation}/…`), applied at construction so segment paths, tmp files, audio rendition dirs and the LRU walk all inherit the namespace. A pod on the old rule cannot address a path in the new generation's tree at all, so the split is unrepresentable rather than a matter of overlap timing. The wipe is deleted: the previous generation is a live pod's working set during the overlap and is now reclaimed only after ten minutes untouched, comfortably longer than a drain. That also removes the cliff the wipe caused — measured the same night, 21.6 GiB discarded at once, every stream cold, client waits to 11.8 s — where a placement change now costs a gradual refill. Reclaim considers only directories, which keeps sparing the persisted rate store beside them (re-probing MOVES placement, so deleting the rates would cause the re-placement the cleanup is tidying up after).
B197|2026-08-16|B196's OWN CLEANUP DELETED THE LIVE CACHE ON ITS FIRST DEPLOY. B196 stopped the cache wiping itself on a generation change by rooting each generation in its own subtree, and added `reclaim_stale_generations` to remove a previous generation once nothing had written to it for ten minutes. The reclaim's rule was "any DIRECTORY in the base that is not the current generation", which is true only once the base contains nothing but generation directories. On the first boot after B196 it does not: the base still holds the PRE-B196 layout, one directory per media id plus `_audiohls`. Deploying 8fb2c5b at 20:29Z the new pod logged `"reclaiming a cache generation nothing has written to", "generation":"4956781705437454830"` — a media id — followed by `"_audiohls"`, and removed the entire cached tree (180 Black Panther segments and every audio rendition) while the outgoing pod was still Running and serving from it. Precisely the wipe B196 exists to prevent, performed by B196's cleanup, at the one moment two pods share the disk. Nothing was lost that was still being watched (the tree had been idle 14 minutes and the grace spared anything fresher), so the cost was one cold cache rather than a broken stream — but the mechanism is exactly the mid-deploy destruction of a live working set, and the grace is a timing accident, not a guarantee. CAUSE: a rule stated over what a directory IS NOT. FIX: `is_generation_dir` — a directory is reclaimable only when its NAME is one `compose_generation` could have produced with a fingerprint (`<digits>-<16 hex>`). Everything unrecognised is left alone. Deliberately, the bare-version form (`"17"`, produced when no placement digest is installed) is NOT recognised either: a media id is also all digits, so treating a numeric name as a generation is the whole bug. That leaks a bounded amount of disk in a configuration production does not run; deleting another process's working set is unbounded harm. Guarded by V150 and by `reclaim_never_touches_a_directory_that_is_not_a_generation`, which replays the exact names destroyed, plus `only_a_fingerprinted_generation_name_is_reclaimable`.
B198|2026-08-16|THE PURGE ENDPOINT WOULD HAVE 400'd ON EVERY REQUEST. Caught pre-merge by its own route test, recorded because the class has bitten twice before (B18 season filtering, B20 search). `PurgeSegmentsQuery` was declared `#[serde(rename_all = "camelCase")]` to match the documented `?itemId=&from=&to=`. But `CiQuery` — the extractor every Jellyfin query uses — normalises each incoming KEY to snake_case before parsing, precisely so `ItemId`, `itemId` and `item_id` all bind. Against a camelCase struct the normalised `item_id` matches nothing, serde fails the required field, and the extractor answers 400 for every caller including a correct one. A helper-level test cannot see this: `quarantine_segments` was green throughout. FIX: plain snake_case fields, which is what `CiQuery` documents and what every other query struct does. Guarded by `an_admin_can_purge_a_time_range_of_an_items_segments`, which drives the ROUTE.
B199|2026-08-16|THE SERVER AUTO-SELECTED A PGS SUBTITLE NOBODY ASKED FOR AND BURNED IT, WHICH COSTS MORE THAN REALTIME AND WEDGED PLAYBACK ALL EVENING. Black Panther (media 4956781705437454830), reported as repeated freezes at advancing timestamps — 1h26m54s, 1h29m40s, 1h32m47s, 1h42m29s — each one "fixed" for a few minutes by purging the range, which is what made it look like poisoned bytes. It was not. THE LINE THAT SETTLES IT, live at 21:42:48Z: `subtitle.requested: None, subtitle.resolved: Some(2), subtitle.codec: hdmv_pgs_subtitle, subtitle.delivery: Burn`. The client asked for NO subtitle; `resolve_selected_subtitle` fell back to the container's default-disposition track; that track is PGS; no browser renders bitmap subs, so `decide_subtitle_delivery` correctly answered Burn — and burning re-encodes every frame with an overlay. T110 measured that cost on 2026-08-05: p50 5380 ms / p95 10995 ms to produce a 6000 ms segment, i.e. ABOVE REALTIME at the p95. The encoder cannot stay ahead of the player, the buffer starves, the client restarts, and every restart asks for more burns. Signature over 15 minutes: 2765 segment serves across 71 unique indices (a 38.9x refetch factor, segment 929 served 118 times), 22 distinct PlaySessionIds, 73 SyncPlay hydrations — while every server-side signal read GREEN (all 200s, sub-millisecond cache reads, no errors, no frame deficits) because a spinning client makes a cache look perfect. Every artefact probed was clean: IDR-led, exact frame counts, on-grid, one encoder per rendition (T118 quarantine + T122 provenance, both landing the same evening). The hazard was documented in the selection log's own comment — "a client that omits the field instead of sending -1 can never remove its subtitles" — and only LOGGED, never prevented. FIX: an IMPLICIT selection may no longer commit to a burn. Narrowed twice so it fixes this without undoing older decisions: by CODEC, an unrenderable ASS default is still burned (B104 — WebVTT conversion drops positioning and fonts, black bars on Android — and a text overlay is far cheaper); by FORCED, a default+forced image track is still burned (B44 — Avatar's Na'vi sections played unsubtitled otherwise, and forced tracks cover a fraction of the runtime). What is declined is the UNFORCED image default, a whole-film burn nobody requested. Where the file also carries a track the client renders itself, that one is selected instead, so the viewer keeps subtitles at zero encoding cost. An EXPLICIT request for an image track is honoured exactly as before — burning what someone chose is the right answer to an expensive request. New counter `pharos_subtitle_autoselect_total{outcome="default"|"swapped_to_external"|"declined_burn"}`. Guarded by V152.
B200|2026-08-16|THE PER-SEGMENT BURN GATE FLIPS THE ENCODER MID-STREAM, SO ONE SESSION'S SEGMENTS ARRIVE UNDER TWO H.264 PROFILES. Regression introduced the same evening by B195. `maybe_gate_burn` (hls.rs) clears `burn_subtitle_stream_index` for any segment whose window contains no subtitle event — correct and valuable on its own, since painting nothing costs what painting something costs. B195 then made a BURNING rendition prefer hardware. Composed, the gate no longer toggles an overlay, it toggles the ENCODER: a segment with dialogue burns on `Nvenc:0` and emits H.264 **Main**, while the quiet segment beside it is gated off, takes the CPU, and emits **High** — under ONE init and one `avcC`. That is precisely what #114 forbids (libx264 and NVENC emit SPS differing in slice-header determinants; a segment from the other encoder is undecodable under seg0's configuration), and the gate flips it every time the film goes quiet. EVIDENCE, 2026-08-16 21:20-22:10Z, Black Panther (media 4956781705437454830): one `PlaySessionId` served both variants — `2fd27db1` burned for segments 1120-1187 then NOT burned for 1188; `d2892ed4` burned 1120-1197 and not burned 1188-1191. Probed from the T118 quarantine: burn variants are Main profile, no-burn variants High, frame counts exact and IDR-led on both sides — each segment individually perfect, the SEQUENCE undecodable. Client symptom is a freeze and an unbounded refetch: segment 1188 served 152 times, segment 929 118 times, a 31-39x repeat factor across 15-minute windows, 22 play sessions, while every server-side signal read GREEN (all 200s, sub-millisecond cache reads, no errors, no frame deficits). Reported by the viewer as "stuck flickering between 2 frames" at 1h26m54s, 1h29m40s, 1h32m47s, 1h42m29s, 1h58m47s and 2h03m05s — six separate timestamps that are not six bad segments but six places where the subtitles stopped. NOT the earlier hypotheses, both of which the evidence killed: not poisoned bytes (every artefact probed clean) and not burn cost (T110's above-realtime figure was measured on CPU in August; tonight burn runs on NVENC at p50 2038 ms against a 6000 ms budget). FIX: placement must not depend on a per-segment flag. Pin the device per RENDITION so every segment of one session comes from one encoder regardless of whether that particular six seconds carries a subtitle — #114's actual rule, which B195 applied to the rendition's INTENT rather than to its identity. Guarded by V153.


B201|2026-08-28|A CHAPTER TITLED FOR A BOUNDARY, NOT A SPAN, WAS TREATED AS A SECOND SPAN OF THE SAME TYPE. `build_media_segments` (system.rs) turns any chapter whose title contains "intro"/"outro"/etc into a full MediaSegmentDto spanning that chapter's own start..end (T86/ADR-0018 #6: chapter-authored spans win over detection for their Type). The Chilling Adventures of Sabrina S01E02's rip carries exactly two chapters, both author-titled around the SAME event: "Intro start" (0-102853ms, the real intro) and "Intro end" (102853ms-3429760ms, i.e. the entire rest of the episode) -- the ripper named the chapter AFTER the cut to describe the transition, not to claim its span is itself an intro. `classify_chapter_title`'s substring match (`contains("intro")`) does not distinguish the two, so both chapters were emitted as "Intro" segments: one correct (0-102853ms), one spanning virtually the whole runtime. jellyfin-web's `MediaSegmentManager` skips the first correctly, then immediately finds the playhead inside the second bogus "Intro" span, so the Skip Intro OSD never disappears for the rest of the episode. Reported as "skip intro skips fine, but then the button never goes away." NOT a detection bug: S01E01 has no chapters at all and is unaffected; the missing Skip Outro on both S01E01 and S01E02 is the separate, already-known T106 consensus-clustering gap (season.rs), not this defect. FIX: keep only the FIRST chapter matching each Type; a same-Type repeat is evidence of a boundary marker, not a second span. Guarded by V154.

B202|2026-09-06|A READINESS GATE EMPTIED INSIDE ITS COMMAND'S LEAD WINDOW NEVER RESOLVED UNTIL THE ANTI-WEDGE DEADLINE. `release_gate_holds_for` (group.rs) drops a member that left or lost its socket from the gate's pending set and resolves only when the set is empty AND the gated command's `not_before` has passed (B38: an ack inside the lead window is spurious). When the LAST holdout dropped inside that window the set was empty but premature, and nothing re-checked it later: `ack_gate` resolves only on `was_pending && remaining == 0`, and a member that was never pending can never satisfy `was_pending`. So the gate sat for its full timeout -- 5 s for a seek, 30 s for a queue change -- and `pharos_syncplay_gate_total{outcome="anti_wedge"}` counted a hang that was really a bookkeeping gap. Found by audit, not live; reachable whenever jellyfin-web's constant socket reconnects land in the ~200 ms after a seek in a small group. FIX: when the pending set empties prematurely, pull the gate's deadline in to the schedule instant; the timer arm resolves an EMPTY gate as `resolved`, and only a gate with holdouts left is `anti_wedge`. Guarded by V155.

B203|2026-09-06|EVERY PAUSE REWOUND THE ROOM BY THE SCHEDULING LEAD. `freeze_paused_position` (group.rs) froze the group at `current_position_ms()` -- the model's position at the instant the Pause was ISSUED -- while the broadcast carried `at_server_ms = now + lead`. jellyfin-web's `schedulePause` executes the command AT `When`: it pauses, then seeks to the command's PositionTicks. So every client played on for the full lead (200 ms with no RTT samples, up to 2.2 s with a slow member) and then seeked back by exactly that much; a late joiner was hydrated to the rewound frame too. The same freeze fed the V19 buffering Pause and the leader/shared pause alike. Found by audit: the C fix on `start_playing` (anchor at `at_server_ms`, not `now`) had already made the Play side honest, and the Pause side was the mirror image left unfixed. FIX: freeze at the position the model reaches at the Pause's own `at_server_ms` (`position_at_ms`), so the seek jellyfin-web performs after pausing is a no-op and the stored paused position is where the room actually stopped. Guarded by V156.

B204|2026-09-06|A GATE RESTORED FROM A SNAPSHOT WAS RE-ARMED ON THE 30 S TIMEOUT WHATEVER ITS REASON. `apply_persist` (group.rs) decoded the persisted gate reason (so metrics stayed honest) but set `deadline = now + READY_TIMEOUT_MS` unconditionally, so a seek gate taken over mid-deploy ran six times the bound B182 set for a seek, and a buffering-unpause gate six times B137's. Found by audit. FIX: the restored deadline comes from `reason.timeout_ms()`, the same table a freshly opened gate uses; `Restored` (an unknown label from a newer build) keeps the long bound because it cannot know which it was. Guarded by V157.

B205|2026-09-06|A CROSS-REPLICA BUS PUBLISH THAT FAILED LEFT NO TRACE. Both egress tasks -- `BusDelivery` (owner -> member, bus_delivery.rs) and `BusCommands` (non-owner -> owner, sync_distributed.rs) -- discarded the `publish` result with `let _`. `PgSyncBus` refuses any payload over 7000 bytes (Postgres NOTIFY caps at 8000), and a `BusMsg::Deliver` carrying a `PlayQueue` runs ~90 bytes per entry, so a queue of roughly 75+ items to a member on the OTHER pod during a rolling deploy was dropped with nothing logged and nothing counted; a large `SetNewQueue` forwarded to the owner met the same fate. The member on the other pod then sat on a stale queue with no server-side evidence of why. Found by audit; the cap itself was known (the local-first short-circuit exists because of it) but the failure path was silent, which is the rich-on-success/silent-on-failure shape §ODD names. FIX: log the drop with the member, message kind, byte size and error, and count `pharos_syncplay_bus_publish_failed_total{kind}` (`deliver` | `command`). The cap itself is unchanged and remains a known gap for deploy windows. Guarded by V158.

B206|2026-09-06|A MEMBER PLAYING INTO A PAUSED GROUP WAS NEVER PULLED BACK. `MemberReady` (group.rs) healed one direction only: a Ready arriving while the group is PLAYING re-sent the member the live Play (the "no active player" race, where a slow-to-load player dropped the broadcast). The mirror -- a member whose player is PLAYING while the group is PAUSED, because it dropped the group's Pause in the same race -- was left alone on the reasoning that healing a paused member re-triggers its Ready in a loop. That reasoning conflated two members: jellyfin-web's `ReadyRequestDto` carries `IsPlaying`, which the server parsed and threw away, so it could not tell the settled member from the runaway one. The reachable shape is the seed-only promote: a solo creator presses pause, the promote reloads its player from a Playing queue, the Pause lands before the player is active and is dropped, and the creator plays on while the group (and every later joiner) is paused. Found by audit. FIX: carry `is_playing` through `ReadyBody` -> `GroupMsg::MemberReady` -> `RemoteCommand::MemberReady` (serde default false for older peers) and, when the group is Paused and the member reports playing, re-send it the group's Pause; counted by `pharos_syncplay_ready_heal_total{group_state="paused"}`. A paused member reporting into a paused group stays silent, which is what keeps the loop closed. Guarded by V159.

B207|2026-09-06|A COMMAND SENT TO A GROUP ACTOR THAT HAD TERMINATED WAS A SILENT 204. `dispatch` (syncplay.rs) discarded the result of `h.tx.send` on the live-socket path, so a session whose hub entry still pointed at a dead actor (the group emptied -- last member pruned or the group dissolved -- while this socket was between reconnects, which keeps the hub entry and its handle) answered every Pause/Seek/Unpause with 204, applied nothing, logged nothing, and counted it `dispatched`. The client stayed in SyncPlay mode with controls that did nothing; the B168 `GroupLeft` heal covers only a LIVE actor that no longer knows the member. Sibling gap: `recover_and_apply` applied a command through a persisted membership without re-attaching the session's hub entry, so until the socket's own B24 re-attach ran, the group's replies to that socket were translated with no group id and a zero epoch (`When` in 1970). Found by audit. FIX: a failed send detaches the dead handle, pushes `NotInGroup` down the socket and counts `pharos_syncplay_command_total{outcome="actor_down"}`; recovery attaches the hub entry before sending. Guarded by V160.

B208|2026-09-06|THE V19 FREEZE PAUSE WAS SENT TO THE MEMBER THAT WAS BUFFERING. `BufferingStart` (group.rs) froze the group with `broadcast(Pause)` -- every member, the stalled one included. jellyfin-web only reports Buffering after 3 s of `waiting`, and its `schedulePause` pauses the element at `When`; a paused element that is mid-load never fires `playing`, and `playing` is the ONLY event that posts `/SyncPlay/Ready`. So the stalled member could never lift the freeze it started: the room sat until `BUFFERING_MAX_MS` (30 s, mis-counted as `recovered` -- B209) or until a human pressed play, after which the same member stalled again minutes later. Live 2026-09-06 17:54-18:00 on group 58ccce5a: member e08fb57b froze the room at 17:54:48, 17:57:30 and 18:00:03; each lift was `superseded` by the same person's Unpause after 3.4-5.3 s with `still_buffering=1`, i.e. "something keeps pausing it and I have to keep unpausing" -- and at 17:18 a SOLO member froze its own group for 30 001 ms. Same shape on 2026-08-28 (17:38, 21:57) and 2026-08-24. Real Jellyfin sends the freeze Pause with `SyncPlayBroadcastType.AllReady` -- everyone except the buffering session (`WaitingGroupState`, "Send pause command to all non-buffering sessions"). FIX: `broadcast_except(member_id, Pause)`. The stalled member keeps loading, fires `playing`, posts Ready, and the resume (B210) brings it back in line. Guarded by V161.

B209|2026-09-06|A BUFFERING FREEZE LIFTED BY ITS DEADLINE WAS COUNTED AS `recovered`. The anti-wedge arm of the actor loop (group.rs) cleared every buffering flag and called `resume_after_buffering`, which attributed the lift as `FreezeOutcome::Recovered` unconditionally; `AntiWedge` was only ever recorded on the branch where a readiness gate was open at the same time. So `pharos_syncplay_buffering_freeze_total{outcome="anti_wedge"}` stayed at zero while the freezes it exists to count ran their full 30 s: `outcome="recovered", held_ms=30001` on 2026-09-06 17:18:58, and `held_ms=30000` twice on 2026-08-28. The dashboard said the freezes healed; the room said otherwise. Found while reading the lines for B208. FIX: `resume_after_buffering` takes the outcome; the deadline arm passes `AntiWedge`. Guarded by V162.

B210|2026-09-06|A MEMBER THAT RECOVERED FROM ITS OWN STALL WAS LEFT BEHIND THE ROOM. When the last buffering member posted Ready, `resume_after_buffering` (group.rs) started the room at its FROZEN position on the ordinary lead. But that member is already playing again, from where it stalled -- behind the frozen position by the stall itself, jellyfin-web's 3 s Buffering threshold and any stream reload -- and its Ready carried that position (`PositionTicks`), which the handler discarded (`position_ms: _`). jellyfin-web's `scheduleUnpause` seeks only a player that is AHEAD of the Play, and sync correction is off by default, so the member stayed those seconds behind for the rest of the item (or until it stalled again). Real Jellyfin delays every OTHER session's Unpause by the lag ("Session is recovering, group will resume in {Delay} seconds"). Latent until B208, which is what lets a stalled member recover by itself at all. FIX: MemberReady passes its reported position when `is_playing`; the resume is scheduled `max(lead, frozen - reported)` out (capped at `MAX_RESUME_CATCH_UP_MS` = 5 s), so the recovering member reaches the frozen position as the room unpauses; `pharos_syncplay_resume_catch_up_seconds` records the wait. Guarded by V162.

B211|2026-09-06|`/System/Endpoint` TOLD EVERY CLIENT IT WAS IN THE NETWORK. `system_endpoint` (system.rs) answered `IsInNetwork: true, IsLocal: true` unconditionally. jellyfin-web reads `IsInNetwork` to pick between the user's HOME and INTERNET streaming-quality settings, so a WAN viewer was handed the home profile; on "Auto" its bitrate probe measured a fast burst, chose >=25 Mbps, and jellyfin-web's hls.js then capped `maxBufferLength` at 6 s on Chrome/Firefox (its high-bitrate workaround for hls.js#876) -- one segment of headroom on the link that most needed more. Seen 2026-09-06: jana (75.161.182.180, Chrome) fetching one 6 s segment every 6 s with nothing buffered ahead, stalling on any single slow segment. PlaybackInfo already knew she was remote (B81's `client_is_remote` caps her transcode at 6 Mbps); the two surfaces disagreed about the same request. FIX: derive `IsInNetwork` from the same `realip_remote_addr` rule PlaybackInfo uses, `IsLocal` from loopback; an unparseable address stays in-network, as B81 chose. Guarded by V163.

B212|2026-09-06|THE SUBTITLE BATCH DEMUX DISCARDED FFMPEG'S VERDICT AND RETRIED EMPTY TRACKS FOREVER. `pre_extract_subtitles` (subtitles.rs) spawned ffmpeg once per item for every not-yet-cached text track, then read each output file; it never looked at the exit status or stderr, so a source that failed to OPEN produced thirty per-track "produced no output" WARNs and not one line naming why -- 7 477 such lines in the week to 2026-09-06, the 2026-09-04 burst being a stale NFS handle (`Error opening input: Stale file handle`) that only the segment path happened to log. And a track that genuinely has no cues was never cached, so it stayed "pending" and every warm pass (each PlaybackInfo prewarm included) re-demuxed the whole multi-GB file to learn nothing again: 60 lines for one item in two days. FIX: log ffmpeg's exit and stderr tail once per batch; when ffmpeg exited cleanly, store an EMPTY entry for a track that produced nothing so it is not re-demuxed until the file changes; a failed run memoises nothing. Guarded by V134 (retrying an answer that cannot change is a leak) and V164.

B213|2026-09-06|TRICKPLAY RE-DECODED UNDECODABLE FILES ON EVERY SWEEP PASS. `generate_item` (trickplay_backfill.rs) logged "trickplay generation failed" and moved on; nothing remembered the failure, so the next pass selected the same item again. Five files libav cannot read ("send packet: Invalid data found when processing input" -- the zeroed/truncated population the probe memo already stops the PROBE retrying) were each attempted 34 times in the two days to 2026-09-06, 2 130 attempts in the week, every one a background-I/O permit and a multi-GB NFS read spent on an answer that cannot change while the file does not. FIX: an in-memory failure memo keyed on (item, mtime, size); a failed item is skipped until its file changes or the process restarts. Guarded by V134.

B214|2026-09-06|THE INTEGRITY SWEEP SCANNED EBOOKS WITH LIBAV ON EVERY PASS. `scannable` (integrity_backfill.rs) admitted anything with a local path, books included; libav cannot open an epub/azw3, the scan failed, and a scan FAILURE is deliberately not memoised (an infrastructure fault must not mark a file unexamined forever) -- so every ebook failed again next pass: 19 002 "scan failed; will retry next pass" lines in the week to 2026-09-06, each costing a background-I/O permit. FIX: only Movie/Episode/Audio are offered to the scan; a book carries no stream libav could judge (004-books FR-001). Guarded by V134.

B215|2026-09-06|THE BELOW-REALTIME ALARM FIRED ON TAIL SEGMENTS. `produce_segment` warned "far below realtime" when a segment took more than three times its own duration; a tail segment can cover a few dozen milliseconds, so any encode at all tripped it (1.4 s for a 0.104 s tail, 2026-09-05) and the alarm counted stalls no viewer experienced. FIX: floor the budget at 1 s. Observability only.

B216|2026-09-06|A TOKEN STORE THAT COULD NOT ANSWER WAS REPORTED AS A BAD TOKEN. Both auth extractors (auth_extractor.rs) mapped every `resolve`/`get` error to `401 invalid token` / `401 user revoked`, `AuthError::Backend` included. At 18:49-18:50Z on 2026-09-06 the API server went away under the CI build (the T128 shape); CNPG lost its lease, the pool waited 25-29 s per connection, and for sixteen seconds every request from every member -- `/SyncPlay/Pause` x5, `/SyncPlay/Buffering` x3, video, audio and trickplay segments -- was answered 401 with "invalid token" logged. The pauses were dropped (the client pauses locally first, so the room desynced) and each client was told its credentials were bad when nothing about them was. FIX: `AuthError::Backend` answers 503 with the store's error in the body; only a rejected token is 401. Guarded by V165.

B217|2026-09-06|A READER WAITED 12 S ON AN AUDIO SESSION THAT HAD ALREADY DIED. `audio_hls_file_budget` (hls_cache.rs) polls the rendition directory and gives up as `never_started` after the no-progress grace; the task that spawned the session's ffmpeg saw it exit non-zero at 20:16:06Z on 2026-09-04 (a stale NFS handle on open) and only logged it, so seven readers each sat the full 12 000 ms before their 404 while nothing could ever be written. FIX: a failed or unspawnable session is recorded per rendition directory (in memory, 30 s), a new session clears it, and a waiting read gives up at its next poll as `session_failed`. Guarded by V165.

B218|2026-09-06|A SOURCE THAT COULD NOT BE OPENED WAS A 500 PER RETRY, AND A FRESH FFMPEG PER 500. During the 2026-09-04 NFS stale-handle event, a Firefox viewer's `h264cmaf/init.mp4` request failed with `Error opening input: Stale file handle`; the segment cache reported it as `Transcode`, the handler answered 500, hls.js retried about once a second and jellyfin-web restarted the session on top, and every retry registered a new encode that spawned ffmpeg against the same dead path: 3 862 `500`s on one item's init in the 20h hour, 2 503 on that path in the week, each one counted as an encode failure. DirectPlay already classified the same condition as `source_unreadable` (404 + `pharos_source_unreadable_total{reason}`); the segment path did not. FIX: an ffmpeg failure anchored on `Error opening input` is re-classified `HlsCacheError::SourceUnreadable{reason}` (stale_handle/missing/permission/io), counted on the DirectPlay series, answered 404, and remembered per source for 5 s so the retry loop is refused without a spawn. Guarded by V166.

B219|2026-09-06|THE PREFETCH DEPTH WAS SIZED FOR THE GPU ON A RENDITION PINNED TO THE CPU. `spawn_segment_prefetch` (hls.rs) chose the hardware depth (`budget - 2`, up to 6 ahead) whenever the server had a GPU that could encode the codec; but a shared-init fMP4 rendition is placed by `DeviceTable::rendition_device`, a hash over the WEIGHTED device spread (007), and a third of browser renditions land on the CPU by design. Such a rendition then warmed six segments ahead on the CPU's four permits beside the segment the viewer was waiting for: on 2026-09-06 a CPU-pinned viewer's live segment ran with `peer_jobs 5`, 3.4 s against 1.2 s alone. FIX: the scheduler exposes the pin (`TranscodeScheduler::pinned_device`, the same pure resolution `candidates_for` applies) and the prefetch depth follows it -- CPU depth for a CPU pin, the session budget for a hardware pin, the old codec-capability heuristic only when nothing is pinned. Guarded by V167.

B220|2026-09-06|CHAPTER THUMBNAILS WERE A COLD FFMPEG SEEK PER REQUEST, AND A CHAPTER LIST REQUESTS THEM ALL AT ONCE. `serve_chapter_image` (images.rs) extracts on miss through the libav worker pool -- a seek into the source over NFS, the same pool live playback uses -- and nothing warmed them ahead of time, so the first open of a chapter list was N concurrent cold seeks. On the Android TV app 64 of 133 `Images/Chapter` requests in the week to 2026-09-06 (48%) were abandoned by the client (499) before the frame arrived. FIX: the priority worker warms the actively-watched item's chapter thumbnails (seed only, gate-paced, capped at 48, stops after 3 failures) so the list is warm by the time it is opened; library-wide warming was rejected as tens of thousands of seeks for lists nobody opens. `pharos_chapter_warm_total{outcome}` counts it. Observability/UX.
B221|2026-09-06|THE LIBRARY BACKFILL REWROTE EVERY ROW ON EVERY BOOT. `backfill_library_ids` ran one unconditional `UPDATE media_items SET library_id = $1 WHERE path LIKE root` per library, shortest root first (B174), so on each start -- and after every rescan delta and remote add -- it rewrote all 26 125 rows (the containing root wrote the nested libraries' rows too, then the nested step wrote them back): sqlx `slow statement` 1.4-3.6 s per library, three times on 2026-09-06 alone, holding row locks against the scan's own batch UPDATEs and the `user_data` upserts of anyone watching. FIX: each step writes only rows whose value would change (`IS DISTINCT FROM` / `IS NOT`) and excludes the paths of the libraries nested under it, so a row is written by exactly the library that keeps it and a repeat run writes nothing; the result reports `changed` beside `assigned` and boot logs `items_restamped`. Performance.
B222|2026-09-06|THE INIT-SEGMENT REFETCH ALARM FIRED ON ORDINARY SEEKING. T126's detector reports a play session that re-requests one segment five times, and the init sentinels (`INIT_SEG_*`, added for the 2026-08-29 wedge that fetched `h264cmaf/init.mp4` 291 times in 27 s) shared that threshold -- but hls.js re-fetches the init with the first fragment after every seek, so a viewer scrubbing an episode crossed it in four seeks: 2508 of 2540 sessions in the week to 2026-09-06 fetched it twice, the healthy tail reached 35, and the WARN fired ~25 times a week for `count=5` sessions that were fine, crying wolf on the one signal that exists to catch a silent wedge. FIX: init sentinels use their own threshold (`INIT_REFETCH_THRESHOLD` = 60, above the observed healthy tail and well below the wedge); media segments keep 5. Observability.
B223|2026-09-06|A "TEMPORARY" DIAGNOSTIC LOGGED 494 WARNS A WEEK ABOUT A WORKING PATH. The B72-era header audit in `extract_token` (gated on `PHAROS_LOG_ALL_REQUESTS=1`, which the deployment has had on since the B68 Android TV diagnosis) warned on every token-less `/stream` request to show what auth material the native ExoPlayer client sent. The answer has been known since B75/B86: it sends none (`okhttp/4.12.0`, no Authorization, no cookie) and the request is authorised by the `?tag=` capability and served 200/206 -- 42 of 42 in the day to 2026-09-06. The audit had no question left to answer and sat fifth in the week's WARN histogram, above every real fault. FIX: removed; the token extractor returns `None` silently and the stream route's own authorisation decides. Observability.
B224|2026-09-06|A GROUP COMMAND DROPPED ON A FULL MEMBER SINK WAS INVISIBLE. `MemberSinks::send` uses `try_send` so a stalled socket cannot block the actor (V19), but the drop discarded the message with `let _` and the comment promised a reconciliation ("via the next catch-up") that nothing schedules: a member whose 64-deep outbound queue is full misses Pause/Play/Seek/PlayQueue and stays out of sync until its socket is declared lost and it reconnects. No metric, no log line -- the July 2026 audit's finding F1, deferred for want of evidence that it happens, which this instrumentation now supplies. FIX (instrumentation first, ODD): `pharos_syncplay_sink_dropped_total{kind,reason}` and a WARN naming the member and message kind. Re-delivery is a separate change once the counter shows the shape. Observability.
B225|2026-09-07|THE VP9 DEMUXED-AUDIO ROUTE HAD NO SIGNAL FOR WHETHER A SESSION EVER GOT REAL AUDIO. `vp9_audio_file` serves both `init.mp4` and the `aN.m4s` fragments for the continuous-audio rendition; only the init half called `note_segment_serve`/`record_audio_init_shape` (B186's own diagnostic), so a session stuck re-requesting init.mp4 and a session playing cleanly were both a stream of 200s with no way to tell them apart from telemetry -- diagnosing a live "it's freaking out at 23s" report during a group watch meant reading raw traces and cross-referencing `session_start_seg` against the shrink-empty-edits guard by hand. Surfaced 2026-09-07 while investigating a Code Geass SyncPlay session; the segment cache purge that would fix a genuinely bad cached segment did nothing because the actual defect (if there was one) would have reproduced on every fresh encode, and nothing said whether the audio side had ever produced a byte for that session. FIX: `note_session_start`/`note_segment_serve` now fire for the real `aN.m4s` fragments too, under a `vp9-audio` surface distinct from `vp9-audio-init` -- `pharos_hls_session_start_total{surface="vp9-audio"}` and the `hls: play session first segment` log line now exist for this route, so "init served repeatedly, first-audio-segment log absent" is a query instead of a reconstruction. Observability.
B226|2026-10-01|SYNCPLAY AUTO-ADVANCE SKIPPED AN EPISODE IN ~4 OF 16 TRANSITIONS. jellyfin-web's `onPlaybackStopped` resolves `getCurrentUser()` before reading `getCurrentPlaylistItemId()` and posting `/SyncPlay/NextItem`, so when the FIRST member's `NextItem` already landed a new `PlayQueue` before a second member's (or the same member's duplicate) promise settled, the second request's body named the entry the queue was ALREADY on. B37's `pli_is_stale` only rejects a `PlaylistItemId` naming something OTHER than current, so this second request passed the check and advanced again. Production evidence 2026-09-13: alison's `NextItem` at 19:14:26, jana's at 19:14:29 (after her client had already loaded the new item) -- skip -- user pressed Previous at 19:14:35; same-device repeats also seen 8s and 46s into the new episode. Surfaced by `pharos_syncplay_next_item_total{outcome}` (added first, ODD), which showed `advanced` firing twice in a row with no `stale`/`end_of_queue` between them. FIX: `NEXT_ITEM_DEBOUNCE_MS` (10s) -- a `NextItem` naming the current entry (or carrying no `PlaylistItemId` at all) is ignored when that entry became current via ANOTHER `NextItem` less than 10s ago AND the group's position is still below that window. Scoped to `NextItem` advances specifically (`next_item_advanced_at`, distinct from the broader `item_started_at` used for logging) so a legitimate first press right after `SetNewQueue`/`SeedQueue`/`SetPlaylistItem`/`PreviousItem` is never suppressed. `PreviousItem` is deliberately exempt -- a user's undo, not a race. V168. Observability + concurrency.
B227|2026-10-01|SYNCPLAY SEEDED A NEW GROUP FROM A STALE SESSION. `/SyncPlay/New`'s `creator_now_playing` resolves the creator's now-playing item from `SessionRegistry::snapshot()` via `find_map` on the first record matching (user, device) -- but `SessionRegistry` keeps one `SessionRecord` per PlaySessionId forever unless the client's own `/Sessions/Playing/Stopped` names it; a playback that never reports Stopped (a SyncPlay item swap destroying the player, a reload, a crash) leaves a ghost record indistinguishable from the live one, and `snapshot()` sorts by session id (a random UUID) rather than recency. Production evidence 2026-10-01: alison's seed log was IDENTICAL at 19:54:36Z and 20:32:35Z (`item_id=...7ebe5ca05c749b position_ms=5680 is_paused=true`) even though her queue had moved to a different item at 20:30:55 -- the seed resolved the 38-minutes-stale ghost. FIX: `SessionEvent::Started` now evicts any OTHER record for the same (user_id, device_id) (never device alone -- B53's shared-device-id browsers must not evict each other) before inserting, so a device plays one thing at a time; `creator_now_playing` additionally picks the most-recently-active match (via an internal monotonic `activity_at`, never serialized to the wire) as defence in depth for ghosts already in the registry from before this fix deployed. Observability first: the seed log gained `session_id`/`candidates`/`since_activity_ms` and `pharos_session_seed_multiple_candidates_total` counts every seed where more than one record matched, before the eviction landed. Guarded by V169.
B228|2026-08-22|A ZEROED REGION MID-FILE MADE ONE SEGMENT UNSERVABLE FOREVER AND WEDGED A SYNCPLAY GROUP. Thor: Ragnarok (media 7643804359852398774), two viewers, 19:52Z: playback reached segment 930 (5580.0s) and both browsers froze. The source mkv carries ~7.5 MiB of zeroed bytes starting at 5091920906 (`0x00 at pos 5091920906 invalid as first byte of an EBML number`, confirmed by raw dd off the NFS mount — 1 MiB windows near offset 4860 MiB are 100% zeros), destroying video ~5582.4-5600s. Same storage-damage class as B185/the 133 zeroed files; the file decodes cleanly on both sides of the hole. The encode path did exactly what T113/B189 taught it — attempt 0 short ("reached only 2.377s of the requested 6.006s"), attempt 1 with the 45s preroll identically short, then `HlsCacheError::Transcode` → 500 — but only the FINAL window ever got an exemption, so a mid-file hole 500s on every request forever: seg 930 was re-encoded and refused in a loop for 11+ minutes while the clients retried. SyncPlay is the victim, not the fault: both members posted Buffering, the viewer's rescue seek (19:53:14, to 5562s — still before the hole) opened a readiness gate on two members who could never become ready, and only the 5s anti-wedge timeout (B137/V139 lineage, working as designed) kept the group from deadlocking outright. The evening ended with the group frozen either way, because no segment past 929 could exist. The tail exemption was the right observation scoped too narrowly: "the frames are not in the source, and neither a re-encode nor a longer preroll can conjure them" is true of ANY window whose source bytes are gone, not just the last one.|B228/V171. When the retry ladder is exhausted — max-preroll attempt still short or zero-frame on a non-final window — the cache now synthesizes a FILLER segment (black video via lavfi at the rendition's resolution/rate, silence for muxed audio) covering the full window, caches and serves it, so playback and SyncPlay coast over the hole instead of dying on it. The filler is encoded through the same scheduler path under the same rendition identity, so it comes off the rendition's pinned device with the same encoder and profile (V153/#114 hold). Counted as `pharos_segment_produced_total{outcome="filler"}` and WARNed with the window, the shortfall and the source path; provenance sidecar marks it. The damaged file itself is re-acquired — the filler is a degradation, not a repair — and the integrity sweep remains the tool that names damaged files (it verdicted two others the same night; Thor's Movies pass had not run yet).
B229|2026-11-01|PASSWORD GUESSING AGAINST THE LOGIN ENDPOINT WAS UNBOUNDED. `POST /Users/AuthenticateByName` (api/jellyfin/users.rs `authenticate_by_name`) ran `state.auth.authenticate` (argon2) on every request with no rate limit, lockout or delay anywhere, on an internet-reachable endpoint: an attacker could try passwords for any username as fast as the server could hash them, and every failure answered a plain 401 with nothing counted or logged beyond the access line.|V174. A shared `LoginThrottle` (auth.rs) keyed per lowercased username AND per client address (IPv6 on the /64) is checked BEFORE the hash; past `[server.login_throttle]` thresholds (default 10 per user / 30 per IP within 15 min) an attempt is refused 429 with `Retry-After`, the delay doubling per further failure up to a 5 min cap. No account is ever locked, and a correct login clears that user's counter (the address's only decays with the window, so a valid account cannot reset it), so a household mistyping a TV password is held for seconds at most. The check and the count happen under one lock (`LoginThrottle::begin` reserves the attempt as a failure before the hash; `Attempt::succeeded` / `abandon` settle it), so concurrent guesses cannot all pass the check while a hash is running. Every password check goes through `LoginThrottle::verify`, including the CurrentPw check of a self-service password change (which was also unthrottled). With `[server].trusted_proxies` empty the per-address limit is off (startup WARN + `pharos_login_throttle_per_ip_active` 0), because behind an unlisted ingress the whole household shares one address. Zero throttle values fail config load. State is in-memory, per process (several replicas multiply the budget) and bounded (10 000 entries per table). The client address is the TCP peer unless the peer is in `[server].trusted_proxies`, in which case `X-Forwarded-For` is read from the right (client_ip.rs). Every refusal counts `pharos_login_throttled_total{reason=user|ip}` and WARNs with reason, username, client address and retry-after; every failure logs both running counts at INFO. Quick Connect finalize (`AuthenticateWithQuickConnect`) is not a password check and is unchanged; `items.rs` and `system.rs` still resolve the client address with actix `realip_remote_addr()`, which trusts `X-Forwarded-For` unconditionally -- not used for any security decision.
B230|2026-11-01|QUICK CONNECT CODES WERE MINTED FROM A CSPRNG WHOSE FAILURE WAS IGNORED. `generate_code` (quick_connect.rs) did `let _ = getrandom::fill(&mut b)`, so a failing OS RNG left the buffer zeroed and every Initiate returned code `000000` (unique-code retries then looped on the same value), and the modulo of a u64 by 1 000 000 was biased. On an internet-reachable unauthenticated endpoint a fixed, guessable code lets an attacker pre-claim the slot a victim's Authorize would bind to.|V175. Code and secret minting return the RNG error; Initiate is refused with `QcRefusal::RngFailed` (HTTP 503), logged at ERROR with the cause and counted in `pharos_quick_connect_refused_total{reason="rng_failed"}`. Codes are drawn as u32 with rejection above 4 294 000 000 so the reduction is unbiased. The wire formats (6 digits, 32-char hex secret) are unchanged.
B231|2026-11-01|THE QUICK CONNECT PENDING MAP WAS UNBOUNDED. `QcMsg::Initiate` is unauthenticated and every call inserted into `by_secret`/`by_code` until its 5 minute TTL, so a loop of POSTs to `/QuickConnect/Initiate` grew the registry's memory without limit and, past ~1M live codes, exhausted the code space the uniqueness loop depends on.|V175. The registry holds at most `MAX_PENDING` (1024) live requests and `MAX_PENDING_PER_IP` (8) per client address (IPv6 keyed on its /64, address from `client_ip` honouring `[server].trusted_proxies`); the per-address cap applies only when `trusted_proxies` is non-empty, because behind an unlisted ingress every client shares one address (same rule as `LoginThrottle` per_ip). Over either cap Initiate is refused (HTTP 429 with `Retry-After` = seconds until the oldest relevant pending request expires), counted in `pharos_quick_connect_refused_total{reason="global_cap"|"ip_cap"}` and WARNed with the address, live count and cap.
B232|2026-11-01|ANY SIGNED-IN USER COULD ENUMERATE QUICK CONNECT CODES. `/QuickConnect/Authorize?Code=` answered 404 for an unknown code with no limit, so one bearer could walk the 1 000 000 code space inside the 5 minute TTL and authorize (to itself, but bound to a victim device's secret) whichever pending request it hit.|V175. The registry counts unknown-code Authorize attempts per authorizing user over a sliding `PENDING_TTL` window; at `MAX_AUTHORIZE_FAILURES` (10) further Authorize calls from that user are refused (HTTP 429, `Retry-After` = time until the oldest failure ages out) even for a valid code. Successes reset nobody's count. Counted as `pharos_quick_connect_refused_total{reason="authorize_throttled"}` with a WARN naming the user and count.
B233|2026-11-01|A DISABLED USER COULD STILL OBTAIN A TOKEN THROUGH QUICK CONNECT. `authenticate_with_quick_connect` resolved the authorizing user with `stores.get(by).into_user()`, which drops the `UserRecord` and so skips `policy.is_disabled`; an account disabled between Authorize and the finalize (or whose pending request outlived the disabling) was issued an AccessToken, while password login (T68) refuses it.|V176. Finalize checks `record.policy.is_disabled` before issuing and answers 401 (WARN with the user id). Guarded by `finalize_refuses_when_the_authorizing_user_was_disabled` in `tests/it_auth/jellyfin_quick_connect.rs`.
B234|2026-11-01|LOGIN TIMING REVEALED WHICH USERNAMES EXIST. `BuiltinAuth::authenticate` returned `InvalidCredentials` immediately when `lookup_by_name` said `UserNotFound`, while a known user paid a full argon2 verify (disabled users already verified first, T68). The response status and body were identical, but the latency was not, so a remote client timing `POST /Users/AuthenticateByName` (and the native login, which reaches the same `AuthBackend::authenticate` through `LoginThrottle::verify`) could tell registered names from unregistered ones. The CurrentPw check on a self-service password change resolves a known user and is unaffected.|V177. An unknown username now verifies the supplied password against a dummy PHC hash, derived once per `BuiltinAuth` with the same `Argon2` config that hashes real passwords (so parameter changes keep parity), ignores the result and returns `InvalidCredentials`. Each dummy verify counts `pharos_auth_dummy_verify_total`; a unit test asserts an unknown-user login increments it and a known-user wrong password and a correct one do not (no timing assertion, so no flake).
B235|2026-11-01|CHANGING A PASSWORD LEFT THE USER'S EXISTING TOKENS VALID. `change_password` (api/jellyfin/admin.rs, routes `POST /Users/{id}/Password` and `POST /Users/Password`) only called `set_password`, so a thief holding a stolen token kept full access after the victim changed their password: the one action a victim takes to evict an intruder did not evict them. Jellyfin's `UserController.UpdateUserPassword` follows a successful change with `RevokeUserTokens(user.Id, currentToken)`; pharos did not.|V178. After `set_password` succeeds, `services::auth::revoke_other_tokens` revokes every token of the TARGET user except the request's own token when the caller is the target (self change); an admin resetting someone else holds a token that is not the target's, so all the target's go and the admin's stays. Backed by `TokenStore::revoke_sessions_except` (sqlite + postgres, hash match per ADR-0011), which spares API keys (tokens whose `device_id` starts `apikey:`; Jellyfin keeps keys in a separate table that `RevokeUserTokens` does not touch). Open native connections are told through `Revocations` (cause `password_changed`, counted on `pharos_auth_revocation_announced_total`) and the count is logged at INFO (`auth: user tokens revoked`, `user_id`, `revoked`). Tests: `tests/it_auth/password_change_audit.rs` and the store conformance suite. A failure of the revoke after the hash swap answers 500 although the password did change; a retry then revokes.
B236|2026-11-01|A SECOND USER COULD TAKE OVER AN ALREADY-AUTHORIZED QUICK CONNECT REQUEST. `QcMsg::Authorize` unconditionally set `authorized_by = Some(by)`, so after user A vouched for a code, any other signed-in user who knew or guessed it re-authorized the same request and the waiting device was logged in as THEM at finalize (and A's authorization was silently discarded).|V179. The first authorizer wins: Authorize on an entry already authorized by a different user is answered as an unknown code (404) and counts as a failed attempt for that user's guessing throttle (V175); the same user re-authorizing is idempotent. Guarded by `first_authorizer_wins_and_a_later_user_is_told_unknown` in quick_connect.rs.
B237|2026-11-01|QUICK CONNECT COULD REISSUE A LIVE CODE. `unique_code` tried 10 000 draws for a code absent from `by_code` and then fell back to one more unchecked draw; with the code space saturated that returned a code already live, and `by_code.insert` replaced the older request's mapping so its owner's Authorize bound to the newer secret (the B-class collision takeover that V8 forbids).|V175. `unique_code` returns `MintError::CodeSpaceExhausted`; Initiate is refused (`QcRefusal::CodeSpaceExhausted`, HTTP 503), counted as `pharos_quick_connect_refused_total{reason="code_space_exhausted"}` and logged at ERROR. `MAX_PENDING` keeps the state unreachable in practice.
B238|2026-10-18|`Is3D` filter on `/Items` previously hardcoded `1 = 0` for `Is3D=true` and `1 = 1` for `Is3D=false`, and `BaseItemDto`/`MediaSourceInfo`/`MediaStream` lacked `Video3DFormat` (Fixed in `018-3d-media`).
B239|2026-10-18|`FullSideBySide` (`3840x1080`) and `FullTopAndBottom` (`1920x2160`) 1080p 3D media were misclassified as `Uhd4k` (`Is4K=true`) because `Quality::from_dimensions` and SQL `Is4K`/`IsHD` filters checked raw coded frame dimensions without per-eye normalization (Fixed in `018-3d-media`).
B240|2026-10-18|Backdrop, thumbnail, and trickplay extraction on packed SBS/TAB 3D videos rendered squished dual-eye frames instead of cropping and aspect-correcting the left eye (Fixed in `018-3d-media`).
B241|2026-11-01|A FOLDER-RESTRICTED USER SAW EVERY LIBRARY'S NAME. `EnabledFolders` narrowed item queries (`apply_policy_scope`) but not the library list: `/Users/{id}/Views`, `/UserViews`, the two `GroupingOptions` routes and `/Library/MediaFolders` built their list from `library_views`, which ignores the caller, and `/Items/{libraryId}` and `/Items/{id}/Ancestors` resolved a library id the same way, so a user restricted to library A still got library B's name and id.|V184. All of them go through `visible_views`, which drops a library the caller's policy does not allow (`UserPolicy::allows_library`) before building it. Guarded by `jellyfin_feature_user_policy::enforce_enabled_folders_filters_views`.
B242|2026-11-01|CHOOSING A GROUPED EDITION PLAYED THE ITEM'S OWN FILE. PlaybackInfo advertised every edition (a 3D rip, a director's cut) as its own MediaSource with Id `{item}-{edition}`, and jellyfin-web sends the chosen one back as `MediaSourceId` (POST body, stream query, subtitle path segment), but no route read it: `/Videos/{id}/stream`, the HLS master/variants/segments, `/audio/{id}/universal` and the subtitle/attachment routes all loaded the item and served its primary path, so picking "3D HSBS" played the 2D file. The edition's MediaSource also had no TranscodingUrl and reused the primary's streams, and the scanner kept only summary fields for an edition, so nothing could have played it from its own file. Signal: `pharos_media_source_selection_total{requested="alternate",served="primary"}` (added first, ODD).|V185. Each edition keeps its full probe (ADR-0030, PROBE_SCHEMA_VERSION 9). `media_source::select` classifies the request's MediaSourceId and swaps in the edition's path and probe under the item's id, marking the value with `MediaItem::edition`; `SourceGen::for_read` keys an edition's segments, ETags and playlist `s=` by its own path while the primary stays LOCAL (existing cache names do not move). PlaybackInfo asked for one edition answers with that edition alone, negotiated against its own probe, with `MediaSourceId` on its TranscodingUrl; `playback_qs` carries it master → variant → segment; subtitle and attachment DeliveryUrls name the selected source and their routes load through `media_source`. An id naming no edition, or an edition probed before v9, is served the primary (recorded `served="primary"`) rather than refused. Prewarm paths still warm the primary for a session playing an edition.
B243|2026-11-01|ANY USER COULD READ EVERY LIBRARY'S ROOT PATH. `GET /Library/VirtualFolders` took `AuthUser` but no admin check, so a regular or folder-restricted user got every library's name, wire id and on-disk `Locations`, bypassing `EnabledFolders` (V184) and disclosing server paths. Jellyfin gates it with `RequiresElevation`; its POST/DELETE siblings and `/Environment/DirectoryContents` were already admin-only. Fix: `require_admin` on the GET; test `virtual_folders_are_admin_only`. Guarded by V186.
B244|2026-11-01|A FOLDER-RESTRICTED USER COULD FETCH ANY ITEM BY ID. `EnabledFolders` narrowed list queries (`apply_policy_scope`) but no by-id route: `/Items/{id}`, `/Users/{uid}/Items/{id}`, PlaybackInfo, `/Videos/{id}/stream`, `/Audio/{id}/stream` and `/universal`, every HLS playlist and segment, Download, Trickplay, Waveform, Lyrics, MediaSegments, Similar, InstantMix, Ancestors, Collections and the played/favourite writes served an item from a hidden library to anyone who knew its sequential id. Synth Series/Season/MusicAlbum/MusicArtist ids resolved without the policy, and lists built outside a `MediaQuery` (similar, instant mix, playlist entries, a series' episodes/seasons, resume) included hidden items. `authorize_media` only checked that the token resolved, discarding its user, so a disabled user's token also still streamed. Fix: one shared check, `services::access::check_item` / `retain_visible`, on every authenticated by-id path; a hidden item is 404 like a missing one; streams resolve the bearer through `user_for_token`. Signal: `pharos_item_access_denied_total{reason}` plus WARN `item withheld by library policy` with item_id, user, library, route. Tests `it_feature::item_access`. Guarded by V187. Not covered: anonymous routes (image GETs, subtitle files, attachments, DLNA), which carry no bearer — tracked separately.
B245|2026-11-01|ANY USER COULD EDIT METADATA AND MAKE THE SERVER FETCH A URL. `MetadataEditor`, `RemoteImages` (+`/Providers`, `/Download`), `RemoteSearch/Movie|Series|Apply` and `POST|DELETE /Items/{id}/Tags` took `AuthUser` with no admin check; `RemoteImages/Download` fetches a caller-supplied `ImageUrl` server-side and stores it as artwork. Jellyfin gates the family with `RequiresElevation`. Fix: `require_admin` on each; test `metadata_editing_routes_are_admin_only`. Guarded by V188.
B246|2026-11-01|A FOLDER-RESTRICTED USER STILL SAW HIDDEN ITEMS IN CACHE-BUILT LISTS. `/Items/Latest`, `/Shows/NextUp`, `/Artists`, `/Albums`, `/MusicGenres`, `/Items/Counts` and the album/discography shortcut of `/Items` built their answer straight from `list_items_cached`, which holds every library, so titles, overviews and ids from a hidden library were listed and counted though B244 refused each by id. Found by the security review of the B244 fix. Fix: `services::access::visible_items` narrows the cached list to the bearer's libraries before any of these routes reads it. Test `cache_built_lists_leave_out_hidden_libraries` (disarmed: every route lists the hidden items). Guarded by V187.
B247|2026-11-01|A TOKEN SENT AS `?api_key=` WAS LOGGED IN FULL. The request span from `tracing_actix_web::root_span!` records `http.target` with its query string, the error-status WARN prints that span, and every trace exports it, so the bearer token of each element URL (`<img src>`, `<video src>`, subtitle `<track>`) reached Loki and Tempo whenever the response was 4xx/5xx. V8 covered headers only. FIX: `StatusRootSpanBuilder` builds the span itself with `http.target` passed through `obs::redact_query_credentials`, which masks `api_key`, `apikey`, `x-emby-token` and the stream capabilities `tag` and `PlaySessionId` as `REDACTED`; guarded by `redaction.rs::query_token_is_masked_in_the_request_span`. V8.
B248|2026-11-01|DLNA BROWSE LISTED THE WHOLE LIBRARY TO ANYONE. `/dlna/*` (device description, ContentDirectory `Browse`, the media URLs it hands out) took no credential and applied no library policy, and the routes were registered whether or not DLNA was wanted, so on a public deployment any internet client could enumerate every title and fetch it. The protocol has no authentication to add. FIX: the routes are served only when `[server].dlna_enabled` is set (default false; SSDP announces only when both `ssdp` and `dlna_enabled` are on, and WARNs when `ssdp` alone is set), guarded by `it_wire::dlna::dlna_routes_are_off_by_default`. When enabled DLNA stays unauthenticated and unscoped, which is why it is off by default. V190.
B249|2026-11-01|ITEM IMAGES, SUBTITLES AND FONTS ANSWERED ANONYMOUS REQUESTS. The image GET/HEAD routes (item, chapter, list), every subtitle route and the attachment route were registered without an extractor, so anyone could fetch an item's poster, subtitles or embedded fonts by id, and the B244 library policy (which needs a bearer) could not apply to them: a folder-restricted user read a hidden item's artwork and subtitles. FIX: each handler calls `require_bearer` (header, `api_key`, or the V191 cookie) and then `require_item_access`; an anonymous request answers 401 and counts `pharos_item_access_denied_total{reason="unauthenticated"}`, a hidden item 404 under `library_not_allowed`. PlaybackInfo appends the caller's `api_key` to each subtitle and font `DeliveryUrl`. jellyfin-web is covered by the V191 cookie; a native client that fetches images with neither a header nor `api_key` now gets 401 (the Android, Android TV, Swiftfin and Findroid apps were not re-tested for this change). Guarded by `it_feature::item_access::file_routes_refuse_anonymous_and_hidden_requests` and `it_feature::auth_cookie::playback_info_delivery_urls_carry_the_callers_token`. V189.
B250|2026-11-01|THE `JellyfinAuth` COOKIE AUTHENTICATED EVERY ROUTE AND EVERY METHOD. P24 added the cookie as a fallback inside `extract_token`, so it reached every JSON API route and every write, and the stream routes set it from an unverified `?api_key=` value. A browser attaches a cookie to any same-site request, including one from a sibling subdomain's page, which `SameSite` cannot tell apart, so a page on another `*.aliflix` host could make writes as the signed-in user. FIX: `extract_token` reads headers and `api_key` only; `extract_media_token` adds the cookie on GET and HEAD, and only the byte-serving routes (images, subtitles, fonts, streams, HLS, trickplay) read it through `MediaUser` or `require_bearer`. `AuthenticateByName` and Quick Connect set it (HttpOnly, SameSite=Strict, Secure on https, Path=/), logout clears it, and the `api_key` echo is gone. Subtitle, attachment and HLS segment responses are now `Cache-Control: private` (they were `public`), so a shared cache cannot replay a cookie-authenticated 200 to an anonymous request for the same URL. Guarded by `it_feature::auth_cookie`. V191. A browser that signed in before the cookie existed regains it on its next header or `api_key` request that the store accepts and that answers 2xx (`CookieBackfill`, counted in `pharos_auth_cookie_backfilled_total{reason}`), so its posters and subtitles stop 401ing without a sign-out.
B251|2026-11-01|A LIBRARY OF COVERLESS BOOKS ADVERTISED A POSTER IT COULD NOT SERVE. `synth_primary_tag` withheld `ImageTags.Primary` only for an artless AUDIO representative (B149), so a library tile whose representative was a book with no cover still advertised one, and `/Items/{libraryId}/Images/Primary` answered 404 on every home screen. Hidden in jellyfin-web's console while anonymous image requests answered 401; surfaced by console-clean once images authenticated. A book, like a track, has no frame to extract. FIX: the synth path follows `dto::image_tags_for` exactly: only a Movie or Episode advertises Primary without cached art. Guarded by `images::tests::a_library_of_coverless_books_advertises_no_primary`. V95.
B252|2026-11-01|PREWARMS WARMED THE PRIMARY FOR A SESSION PLAYING A GROUPED EDITION. After B242 every request route read the selected edition, but `prewarm_group_seek` and `prewarm_cold_start` loaded the item by id alone (`fetch_item`, no request, so no `MediaSourceId`) and keyed with `item.edition = None`. A SyncPlay group or a resume playing the 3D edition had the primary's segments transcoded at the seek target or resume point, under the primary's key and with the primary's duration, frame rate and bitrate: background encodes nobody fetched, competing with the edition's live segments (the B219 contention shape), while the edition's first segment still cold-spawned ffmpeg. Signal: the prewarm log lines' `prewarm.source="primary"` beside the segment route's `pharos_media_source_selection_total{route="hls",served="alternate"}` (added first, ODD).|V192. The segment-opts hint (`SegmentOptsHint`) records the edition each session's segment request read, and the seek prewarm groups members by edition and loads each through `media_source::load_named`; PlaybackInfo passes the source id it answered with to the cold-start prewarm, which loads the same way. Both record `route="prewarm"` on `pharos_media_source_selection_total`. Guarded by `hls::prewarm_edition_tests`. Open: the VP9 audio rendition (`ensure_audio_hls_covering`) now reads the edition's path, but its directory is `_audiohls/{media_id}-a{a}-b{br}` with no source generation, so an edition and the primary share one audio session.
B253|2026-11-01|AN EDITION AND ITS PRIMARY SHARED ONE VP9 AUDIO SESSION. `HlsSegmentCache::ensure_audio_hls_covering` put the continuous audio rendition in `_audiohls/{media_id}-a{a}-b{br}`, with no source generation. A grouped edition shares its primary's `media_id`, so after B252 made the routes and prewarms read the edition's path, both still resolved to one directory: whichever file's session started first served its audio to viewers of both (a 3D edition with the primary's audio, or the reverse, desynced wherever the cuts differ). Nothing said which file a session read: the open log fired only for seek sessions and named no source. Signal (added first, ODD): the `audio HLS: opening session` line's `audio.source` / `audio.dir` and `pharos_audio_session_opened_total{source}`; before the fix a session for `source="alternate"` opens in a directory with no `-g` suffix, or never opens while `pharos_media_source_selection_total{route="hls",served="alternate"}` climbs.|V193. The directory now appends `SourceGen::for_read(source, edition).key_suffix()` (the suffix segment filenames use): a local primary keeps `{media_id}-a{a}-b{br}`, an edition gains `-g{gen}`. A URL-backed item is non-local under `for_read`, so its directory gains the suffix too and a re-resolved URL opens a fresh session. `edition` is threaded through `ensure_audio_hls`, the rendition playlist and file routes and the SyncPlay pre-seek in `prewarm_group_seek_source`. Guarded by `hls_cache::tests::an_edition_and_its_primary_open_distinct_audio_sessions` (red with the suffix dropped) and `an_audio_session_records_the_source_it_opened`. The MPEG-TS continuous AAC encode for muxed segments (`continuous_audio_root`, `_contaudio/{media_id}-a{a}-b{br}`) had the same sharing, with the wrong audio then cached as immutable under the edition's segment key: its root now appends the generation of the segment it serves (`key.source_gen`), so the two always read one file, and the spawn log names `audio.dir`. Guarded by `an_edition_and_its_primary_read_distinct_continuous_audio` (red with the suffix dropped).
B254|2026-11-02|THE `JellyfinAuth` COOKIE BACKFILL PLANTED ANY ACCEPTED TOKEN, INCLUDING AN `api_key`, AS THE BROWSER'S SESSION. `CookieBackfill` (B250) set the cookie on any 2xx response to a request that authenticated by header or `?api_key=`, and overwrote a cookie holding another token. A page on a sibling `*.aliflix` host could embed `<img src="https://pharos/Items/…/Images/Primary?api_key=ATTACKER_TOKEN">`; the request is same-site, so the browser stored the response's Set-Cookie and the victim's posters, subtitles and streams then authenticated as the attacker (session fixation). The store accepting the token proved it valid for someone, not that it belonged to this browser. Native clients also got a Set-Cookie with their own token on every response. FIX: `note_header_credential` records a header token only (`extract_header_token`), never `api_key`; the middleware sets the cookie only when the request carries `Sec-Fetch-Site: same-origin` and no `JellyfinAuth` cookie, and never overwrites a cookie for another token. Every decision is counted in `pharos_auth_cookie_backfill_total{outcome=set|held|not_same_origin|other_cookie|unencodable}`. Guarded by `it_feature::auth_cookie::{an_api_key_never_sets_the_cookie, a_header_token_from_anywhere_but_the_same_origin_gets_no_cookie, a_cookie_holding_another_token_is_kept}`. V194.
B255|2026-11-02|AN EMBEDDED ATTACHMENT COULD RUN SCRIPT ON PHAROS'S ORIGIN. `deliver_attachment` served an attachment inline with the Content-Type its media file declared, and no response carried `X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff`. A crafted MKV with a `text/html` or `image/svg+xml` attachment, opened at `/Videos/{id}/Attachments/{n}` (with the cookie or `api_key`), rendered as a page on pharos's origin, where its script could read the signed-in user's token from jellyfin-web's localStorage or plant a token as the browser's session. FIX: every response is `nosniff` (`middleware::nosniff`); an attachment is served inline only when its type is a font (SubtitlesOctopus loads fonts by URL), anything else gets `Content-Disposition: attachment`; every attachment carries `Content-Security-Policy: default-src 'none'; sandbox`. Guarded by `it_feature::attachment_headers`. V195.
B256|2026-11-02|A PLAYSESSIONID AUTHORIZED STREAMS FOR ANYONE, AFTER SIGN-OUT, AND FOR HOURS. Since B75 the `tag`/`PlaySessionId` on a stream URL authorizes a tokenless request on its own, so it is a bearer credential, but the session recorded only its media id. Whoever held the id could stream that item after the negotiating user signed out, was revoked or lost access to the library; `/Sessions/Playing/Stopped` left the session in place; any signed-in user could drop another's session through `DELETE /Videos/ActiveEncodings`; a durable row kept authorizing until the hourly prune (6 to 7 hours); and the id was logged whole in the playback decision line, the transcode registry warnings, the session eviction line and the store spans.|V196. A transcode session records its holder (user id and `TokenStore::token_id`, the token's hash; migration 0059), and every capability use re-resolves that token and re-checks the holder's item access (`stream::capability_holder_allowed`); a row with no holder reads as expired. `/Sessions/Playing/Stopped` and `DELETE /Videos/ActiveEncodings` end the session through `TranscodeSessionRegistry::end_held`, only for its holder. `get_transcode_session` skips rows written before now − 6 h, and a capability is always checked against the stored row (`TranscodeSessionRegistry::get_stored`), never a replica's cache, so a stop on any replica and the 6 h limit hold for an id kept warm by polling. Logs carry `pharos_core::secret_prefix` (8 characters). Guarded by `it_playback::jellyfin_playbackinfo_native_directplay::a_capability_*`, `transcode_sessions::tests::a_stored_session_*` `transcode_sessions::tests::the_stored_row_is_the_capability` and `redaction::play_session_id_is_logged_by_prefix_only`. Open: if the store fails to delete the row on a stop, the session stays a capability (until the row's 6 h) and the stop still answers success; the failure is logged at WARN.
B258|2026-11-02|`/Search/Hints` NAMED ITEMS AND PEOPLE FROM LIBRARIES THE USER CANNOT SEE. The handler ignored its user: title hits came from the store's library-wide full-text search, artist, album and genre hints from every stored item, and person, studio, box set and tag hints from the store-wide lists. A user restricted to one library could search out the titles, artists, cast and studios of every other library, and of items above their parental rating.|V197. `search_hints` reads the user's visible items (`services::library::visible_items`, the same `apply_policy_scope` query `/Items` runs; `None` for an unrestricted user, who keeps the old path). Title hits are filtered to that set, with the FTS window widened to the visible corpus so dropped hits do not empty the page; artist, album and genre hints and the empty-term listing are built from it; a person, studio, box set or tag hint is kept only when `/Items?ParentId=` on it lists a visible item (`services::library::parent_has_visible_items`). Guarded by `it_feature::item_access::search_hints_answer_only_visible_items`.
B259|2026-11-02|ANY SIGNED-IN USER COULD READ, CHANGE OR DELETE ANOTHER USER'S PLAYLIST. Playlists record their owner, and the `/Items?IncludeItemTypes=Playlist` listing showed each user only their own, but `GET /Playlists/{id}`, `GET /Playlists/{id}/Items`, `/Items/{id}`, adding, removing and moving entries and `DELETE /Playlists/{id}` ignored the bearer.
